That value is the tail of the doctor's Cognito `sub`, dressed up as a doctor ID: meaningless to a patient and an internal identifier the UI has no reason to publish.

  **The client omitted a header the contract declares required.** `contracts/openapi.yaml` marks `X-Correlation-ID` (`RequiredCorrelationId`) required on `createConsultationDocument`, but the client issued the write through `api.post`, which cannot carry a correlation id, and the handler never read one — so the one operation whose contract demands a caller trace id was the one operation without one.

  **The five permanently-ALARM dev CDS alarms.** ADR-20260804-02 landed `local.idle_tolerant_missing_data` in `infra/modules/cds_operations/monitoring.tf`, resolving `dev` to `notBreaching`, and recorded applying it as a follow-up. It was never applied. Live `dev` still treats missing data as breaching, and the four audit-outbox backlog alarms plus the audit DLQ depth alarm sit in `ALARM` continuously because the environment is idle by design. That follow-up is now revisited on its merits rather than executed by default.
- Decision:
  - **Reject overlapping slots server-side with `409 STATE_CONFLICT`, on both create and update.** A slot occupies the **half-open** minute interval `[startTime, startTime + durationMinutes)`. Two slots conflict when those intervals intersect, which makes back-to-back slots legal by construction: `09:00`+30 ends at the same minute `09:30`+30 begins, so neither claims a minute the other does. `intervalsOverlap` and `findOverlappingSlot` in `backend/src/lib/schedule.ts` hold the arithmetic; the guards in `handleCreateSlot` and `handleUpdateSlot` apply it.
  - **Occupancy is status-agnostic.** `available`, `booked`, and `blocked` all conflict. All three mean the doctor's calendar is committed for that window — `blocked` is time withdrawn from offer, `booked` is time a patient holds. A status-sensitive rule would let a doctor publish availability on top of time they had already blocked out, which is the same double-booking defect wearing a different hat.
  - **Overlap is decided from a strongly consistent base-table read.** `readSlotsForDate` queries `pk = DOCTOR#<id>` with `begins_with(sk, 'SLOT#<date>')` and `ConsistentRead: true`. This is not optional: an eventually-consistent replica that has not yet seen a slot written a moment ago reports a free window and lets the second write through. A GSI could not serve this, since GSIs are eventually consistent only. The cursor walk is bounded to five pages — one date at 15-minute granularity is 96 slots, so a single 1 MB page already covers any real day and the bound exists only to stop a pathological partition looping.
  - **Only the conflicting slot is rejected, not the whole request batch.** The client generates a shift one request at a time and reports per-slot outcomes, so failing everything would discard good slots.
  - **Create uses best-effort post-write reconciliation, not atomicity. Stated plainly because it matters:** read-then-write is not atomic, and two requests with different idempotency keys can both pass the guard and both persist. After its own write, create re-reads the date and, if it now sees an overlapping peer, applies a deterministic tie-break — the smaller `slotId` keeps its slot — so exactly one racer withdraws and the surviving schedule has no overlapping pair. The withdrawal is conditional on the slot still being `available`, so a slot a patient booked in the interim is never deleted out from under them. **Residual window:** between the write and the withdrawal a reader can see both slots, and if the withdrawal itself fails — throttle, Lambda kill, or the slot became `booked` — the overlap survives and the caller gets a `500`, which is the honest answer because the outcome is genuinely unknown to the handler. A retry re-runs the guard and resolves to `201` or `409`. This is convergence, not a transaction.
  - **`PUT` rejects only what its read sees, and does not reconcile.** A status-only or notes-only update skips the read entirely, since it cannot move the window. A time-moving update excludes the slot's own record, so re-sending its current time is not a conflict with itself. There is deliberately **no** post-write reconciliation on the update path: withdrawing an update means writing the old time back, which would clobber whatever a concurrent writer legitimately stored in the meantime. Create is the path that converges after a race; `PUT` rejects what it can see and no more.
  - **Contract updated in the same change set**, per the contract-first rule: both `409` descriptions now name `STATE_CONFLICT` alongside `IDEMPOTENCY_CONFLICT`, and the overlap rule, its half-open semantics, and its status-agnostic scope are stated on both operations. Generated backend and frontend types regenerated.
  - **The client-side overlap check is demoted to a courtesy.** `slotPlan.ts` keeps skipping known overlaps so the preview stays honest and requests certain to be refused are not sent, and its comment now says the backend is the thing that decides.
  - **Delete the twelve unsourced `SearchDoctor` fields and the `Location` interface outright.** `SearchDoctor` now declares `id`, `doctorId`, `name`, `specialty`, `nextAvailable`, and `scheduleSpace` — exactly what `mapDoctorToSearchDoctor` builds from `DoctorPublicSummary` plus real availability slots. Removing the declaration is the point: with no field there is nothing for a future default to be invented *for*, and doctor credentials are the worst possible place to invent one. If the backend ever holds these values, the field is re-added in the same change that sources it.
  - **Guard the type surface, not just the rendered output.** `fabricated-data-scan.test.ts` gains a scan asserting the exact declared field set of `SearchDoctor`, a per-field assertion that none of the twelve returns, and an assertion that no distance-bearing helper type reappears. The existing literal scans catch a fabricated value coming back; this catches the shape that made adding one easy.
  - **Remove the doctor `ID:` row rather than relabel it.** There is no patient-facing doctor identifier on the platform to show in its place. `doctorId` remains in use for fetching and for the booking route — only the display is gone. A new `page.test.tsx` asserts the real name and specialty render and that no substring of the Cognito `sub` does.
  - **Send `X-Correlation-ID` on `createConsultationDocument`**, issuing the write through `api.request` because `api.post` cannot carry the header, generating a PHI-free `cds-<uuid>` when the caller supplies none, and accepting an explicit `correlationId` so the retries of one logical create correlate. The `Idempotency-Key` is still sent — `api.request` auto-generates one for any non-`GET` — and a test asserts that alongside the correlation header.
  - **Handler-side correlation resolution is fail-open.** `resolveCorrelationId` accepts a value matching the contract's own bounded charset (`^[A-Za-z0-9._:-]{1,64}$`) and otherwise falls back to the API Gateway request id. A missing or malformed header degrades the trace id; it does not reject the write. Enforcement stays where it belongs, on the assessment-gated CDS boundary.
  - **Keep the deployed `dev` CDS alarm posture at `treat_missing_data = "breaching"`. No Terraform change in this change set.** The five permanently-`ALARM` alarms in the idle `dev` environment are **accepted noise**. Silencing them would let a genuinely broken CDS path sit undetected: an alarm that never fires cannot distinguish "idle by design" from "the outbox is failing every invocation", and that is not hypothetical — ADR-20260804-02 documents `bayanhealth-dev-cds-audit-outbox` failing 100% of invocations for four days while the unit suite reported green. A false positive that is understood is cheaper than a false negative that is not.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Enforce overlap with a DynamoDB conditional expression instead of a read: rejected as not expressible. The sort key is `SLOT#<date>#<slotId>` and `slotId` is random, so no condition on `pk`/`sk` can say "this window is free". A time-keyed sort key would make it expressible — see follow-ups — but changing the key shape of a live entity is a data-model migration, not a defect fix.
  - Use a DynamoDB transaction across the date's slots: rejected. `TransactWriteItems` conditions target specific items, and the items that would need to be excluded are precisely the ones whose keys are not known without a read. It would not remove the read, only add cost and a 100-item ceiling.
  - Make the create path atomic with a per-doctor-per-date lock item: rejected for now. It closes the residual race, and it also serializes every slot write for that doctor behind one hot item and introduces a lock whose expiry must itself be correct on a Lambda that can be killed mid-request. Best-effort reconciliation plus an honest statement of the residual window is the smaller, less failure-prone change.
  - Skip reconciliation and accept the read-then-write race silently: rejected. The race is narrow but real, and a schedule that can contain a duplicate published time is the defect being fixed, not a variant of it.
  - Reconcile on the `PUT` path too: rejected on the reasoning above — undoing an update means restoring a prior value, which is a lost-update generator, not a fix.
  - Treat only `available` slots as occupying time: rejected. It permits publishing availability over a `blocked` window, which reproduces the defect.
  - Reject the whole shift when any slot overlaps: rejected. The caller creates slots one request at a time and surfaces per-slot results; an all-or-nothing failure throws away slots that were fine.
  - Return `409` for a malformed stored `startTime`: rejected. An unplaceable stored record is skipped rather than treated as a conflict, so one bad row cannot make an entire date unschedulable.
  - Keep the `SearchDoctor` fields as optional and rely on the literal scan: rejected. The literal scan is a trailing indicator — it fires after someone has written a fabricated value. The declaration is the leading one.
  - Relabel the doctor `ID:` row as "Reference": rejected. It would still be the Cognito `sub`, just harder to recognise as one.
  - Make the correlation header fail-closed with `400`: rejected. It would start rejecting writes from already-deployed clients, and a degraded trace id is a much smaller harm than a blocked consultation document.
  - Apply ADR-20260804-02's `notBreaching` change to `dev` as its follow-up anticipated: rejected on reconsideration, and this ADR supersedes that decision bullet for `dev`. The change would have removed the only signal that distinguishes an idle pipeline from a dead one, in the environment where that failure has already happened once. `staging` and `prod` remain `breaching` and are untouched by this reasoning.
  - Raise the alarm thresholds or extend the evaluation periods instead: rejected. The thresholds are correct — any audit DLQ item is significant — and lengthening evaluation windows delays a real signal without removing the standing one.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: a doctor cannot publish two overlapping slots through the API, whatever the client does, and cannot walk an existing slot onto committed time with an update. Back-to-back scheduling still works, and a booked or blocked window is respected.
  - Positive: the overlap rule is stated in the contract, so a second client cannot be written against the old behaviour by accident.
  - Positive: the doctor view model can no longer offer a slot for a credential the platform does not hold, and the scan makes reintroducing one a test failure rather than a discovery in front of a reviewer.
  - Positive: consultation-document creates now carry the correlation id their contract has always required, and no patient sees another party's internal identifier on the doctor detail page.
  - Positive: an `ALARM` in `dev` remains capable of representing a real failure, because nothing was silenced to make the dashboard look clean.
  - Trade-offs: create now costs two extra strongly consistent queries per request — one guard read, one reconciliation read — on top of the write. At 96 items per date worst case this is a single page each, but slot creation is measurably more expensive than before. Bulk shift generation pays it per slot.
  - Trade-offs: the create path can now return `500` in a case that previously returned `201` — specifically when it must withdraw its own slot after losing a tie-break and the withdrawal fails. That is deliberate: the handler does not know the final state, and claiming success would be a lie. Callers must treat `500` on slot create as indeterminate and retry.
  - Trade-offs: the residual race is real and documented rather than closed. Two concurrent creates can be observed as overlapping by a reader in the window between the write and the withdrawal.
  - Trade-offs: `PUT` remains weaker than create by design. A concurrent write landing between its read and its update can still leave an overlapping pair, and nothing repairs it.
  - Trade-offs: the five `dev` CDS alarms stay red, so `dev` alarm state carries no information at a glance and must be read against this entry. The mitigation is that a *change* in the accompanying metric still shows, and the DLQ depth alarm is checked by depth rather than by state.
  - **Not qualified.** This is local evidence: frontend typecheck, lint, 622 tests and a production build; backend typecheck, lint and 890 tests; contract lint and generated-type drift check. It is not staging qualification, not canary authorization or observation, and not production enablement. No environment was mutated, no Terraform was applied, and the overlap guard has not been exercised against a deployed API.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) consider a time-ordered slot sort key (`SLOT#<date>#<startTime>#<slotId>`) which would make overlap expressible as a conditional write and remove the read-then-write race entirely — a data-model change requiring `DATA_MODEL.md` and a migration, so not bundled here; (b) the residual create race and the unreconciled `PUT` race both remain open, and neither is detectable after the fact today — a periodic overlap audit over each doctor's dates would surface one; (c) `features/patient/components/Header.tsx` still renders the signed-in patient's own truncated `userId` as `ID : {shortId}`, left alone because it is a patient seeing their own identifier rather than another party's, and recorded here rather than changed; (d) ADR-20260804-02's follow-up (b) is now closed as *will not apply for `dev`*; its follow-ups (a), (c) and (d) — deploying the `ttl` fix, confirming the outbox error rate returns to zero, and never purging a `staging`/`prod` audit DLQ item — remain open and unaffected.
- Related files: `backend/src/lib/schedule.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/schedules.ts`, `backend/src/__tests__/schedule-overlap.test.ts`, `backend/src/__tests__/security-wave4-preservation.test.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/consultation-documents.ts`, `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `backend/src/contracts/openapi.generated.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/types/openapi.generated.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/components/schedule/slotPlan.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/types/searchDoctors.types.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/app/fabricated-data-scan.test.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/app/booking/doctor/[doctorId]/page.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/app/booking/doctor/[doctorId]/page.test.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/lib/api/consultationDocuments.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/lib/api/consultationDocuments.correlation.test.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/lib/api/consultationDocuments.test.ts`, `infra/modules/cds_operations/monitoring.tf` (unchanged — posture decision only)

### ADR-20260807-01: Atomic slot reservation, a stated platform timezone, a usable clinical-input floor, and three reliability repairs

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally; not qualified in any environment
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: Six defects found on the demo path. Two of them made a documented feature not work at all, one made an ordinary patient behaviour permanently brick a consultation, and one was a money-handling window. They are recorded together because four of them share a single root cause that had never been written down.

  **A slot's wall-clock time had no stated meaning.** `lib/schedule.ts` documented `startTime` as "HH:MM (24h, local to doctor's timezone — stored as-is)" and no timezone was recorded anywhere, on the slot or elsewhere. A booking's `scheduledAt`, by contrast, is an ISO-8601 instant. Every place that had to relate the two therefore invented its own interpretation, and they disagreed: the booking form built a `Date` in the *browser's* timezone, while `matcher.ts` read the instant's **UTC** hours and minutes and compared them against the wall-clock strings directly. A 09:00 Manila slot is 01:00Z, so the matcher asked "does a slot starting at 09:00 cover minute 60?" and the answer was always no.

  **Booking a published slot reserved nothing.** `POST /v1/bookings` accepted a free-form `scheduledAt` and never touched the doctor's schedule. The patient picked a time on `/booking/doctor/{doctorId}`, which navigated to the form with `?slotId=`; that id preselected the time and was then discarded. So the slot stayed `available`, the next patient was offered it, and both got a `201`. `ApiSlot` had declared `bookingId?` "set when status = booked" since the schedule feature was written — nothing ever set it. The collision surfaced only when a human read the calendar.

  **Availability matching was inert in two directions at once.** `doctorHasSlotAvailability` opened with `if (slots.length === 0) return true;` — a doctor with no published availability on the requested date reported as *available*, so the doctor least able to take the booking passed most easily. Because that short-circuited before any time comparison, it also concealed the timezone defect above: a doctor with zero slots passed, and a doctor with exactly the right slot failed.

  **A patient who never filled in the intake form permanently bricked the consultation.** `endCanonicalSessionAndInitializeAssessment` writes an empty Clinical_Input_Head — `status: 'ready'` but `clinicalInputRevision: 0`, no `normalizedInputDigest`, no snapshot item. `ingestIntakeAsClinicalInput` returned `skipped` when there was no submitted intake, and the completion tail in `handlers/payments.ts` logged that and moved on. Every downstream reader (`candidate-service.ts` `loadAuthorities`, `gate-authority.ts` `loadGateAuthority`) requires a revision, a digest, and a matching snapshot, so the physician finished a real consultation and then met `503 RED_FLAG_EVALUATION_FAILED` on every post-consult call, permanently, with no recovery path inside the application. The route in was the most ordinary one there is: book, attend, skip the form.

  **A failed payment write could leave money held against an unconfirmed booking.** `createPaymentHold` took the gateway hold, then issued two sequential writes: put the payment record, then update the booking to `confirmed` under `status = pending_payment`. A booking the patient had cancelled, or that a concurrent request had already confirmed, failed that condition — leaving a persisted `held` payment attached to a booking that was never confirmed, and nothing recording the contradiction. It also read the booking with `booking!` *after* the payment write, so a missing booking became a `TypeError` with the hold already taken.

  **A failed OTL activation locked the doctor out for up to 15 minutes.** Activation is one transaction: consume the link, start the canonical session, move the booking to `in_progress`. When the booking side failed, nothing committed and the OTL stayed `active` while being permanently unusable. Activating again failed identically, and issuing a replacement was refused because `listActiveOtlsForBooking` still counted the dead link. The only exit was waiting out the TTL, mid-consultation.

  **`CDS#POLICY#<env>` absence and deliberate disablement were indistinguishable to operators.** `getActivePolicyBundle` returns a fail-closed sentinel (revision 0, mode `disabled`) for a missing or malformed item and never throws — correct runtime behaviour, and completely silent. Both a never-seeded environment and a deliberately closed gate produce an identical `503 CDS_GENERATION_DISABLED`, which is right at the API boundary and useless to whoever has to fix it.
- Decision:
  - **State the platform timezone once, in one constant, and derive from it everywhere.** `PLATFORM_UTC_OFFSET_MINUTES = 480` in `backend/src/lib/schedule.ts`: slot wall-clock times mean **Asia/Manila**, a fixed UTC+08:00. A bare offset is exact rather than approximate here because the Philippines has observed no DST since 1978, and this is a single-country platform (`ap-southeast-1`, PH payments, Filipino patient-education corpus). `slotStartInstant(date, startTime)` and `candidateSlotDates(instant)` are the only sanctioned conversions; `matcher.ts` and the booking reservation both use them. If BayanHealth ever serves a second timezone this constant is the wrong shape and the slot item must carry its own zone — named here rather than quietly generalized.
  - **`POST /v1/bookings` accepts `slotId` and reserves it in the same transaction that writes the booking.** `TransactWriteItems` puts the booking (`attribute_not_exists(pk)`) and updates the slot to `booked` with `bookingId` set, conditional on it still being `available`. Two patients racing one slot therefore produce exactly one booking and one `409 SLOT_UNAVAILABLE`; the loser's booking is never written, so there is no orphan booking pointing at a slot someone else holds and no slot marked booked by a booking that failed to persist. This is the only cross-partition transaction in the codebase because it is the only place two items must agree or neither may exist.
  - **When a slot is referenced, the slot owns the appointment time.** The stored `scheduledAt` is derived from the slot's `date` and `startTime`; the client's submitted value is used only to choose which slot dates to search, and a disagreement is logged rather than rejected. Same reasoning as server-owned pricing under ADR-20260726-01: the client's instant is a *rendering* of server-held state, built in the browser's timezone, so a patient outside UTC+08:00 submits an instant hours from the slot the doctor published. Rejecting on mismatch would elevate an unreliable input into a gate; deriving removes the browser from the answer. The patient's confirmation screen reads the booking back from the server, so the authoritative time is what they are shown.
  - **`slotId` requires `preferredDoctorId`, and an unknown slot is a `404`.** A slot is keyed under its doctor's partition and cannot be located without one, so the pairing is enforced at validation (`422`). The same `404` covers "belongs to a different doctor", so the endpoint does not confirm that another doctor's slot id exists. The slot is located by a bounded three-date sort-key range rather than a partition scan — see `candidateSlotDates`, whose ±1-day bracket covers every real UTC offset.
  - **Cancelling or rescheduling releases the slot.** Both end the booking's hold on a doctor's published time, so both return it to `available`, conditional on the slot still recording *that* booking — which makes the release idempotent and unable to disturb a slot since re-reserved. Reschedule additionally `REMOVE`s `slotId` so the booking stops claiming a slot it does not hold. Sequenced after the booking update commits, for the same reason the refund is: a concurrent update that loses the `ConditionExpression` must not free a slot its booking still holds. A release failure is logged for reconciliation, not surfaced as a failed cancellation.
  - **`doctorHasSlotAvailability` answers `true` only on evidence.** A slot the doctor actually published, still `available`, containing the booking's platform-local minute. No slots, all booked, all blocked, slots at other times, a malformed row, or an unparseable `scheduledAt` are all `false`. Exported for test, because it cannot be observed through `matchDoctorForBooking` — see the trade-off below.
  - **A consultation with no patient-reported intake gets an explicit "no intake" clinical source, not an empty Head.** `ensureClinicalInputForConsultation` ingests the submitted intake when there is one and `toNoIntakeClinicalContent(reason)` when there is not, through the same approved adapter and normalization, so the Head reaches a non-zero revision with a matching snapshot either way. **The content asserts the absence of screening, never negative findings**: `chestPain`, `dyspnea`, and `feverDays` are absent rather than `false`, `screeningCompleteness` records all three as unanswered, and `intakeAvailability` records why — so an audit can tell "never asked" from "screened negative", which is the entire reason that field exists. `noIntakeSourceId` is distinct from `intakeSourceId`, so a real intake ingested later lands as its own source rather than overwriting the record that screening never happened.
  - **The payment record and the booking confirmation commit together.** One `TransactWriteItems`, both original conditions retained. The booking is read *before* the write instead of between the two writes, and a missing booking is refused with `BOOKING_NOT_FOUND` rather than dereferenced. The gateway hold cannot be inside the transaction, so when the transaction fails the hold is reported as `payment_hold_reconciliation_required` with its provider reference — the only handle anyone has on the money. It is not undone automatically: `processRefund` loads the payment item from `app_core` to find what to refund, and in this path that item was never written.
  - **A failed OTL activation retires the link.** New terminal status `revoked`, distinct from `consumed`: never used, and never usable. The activate route inspects `CancellationReasons` — position 0 is the OTL's own consume-update, so if that held and a later item failed, nothing committed and the link is dead. `revokeOtl` then drops it out of `listActiveOtlsForBooking` and the doctor can issue a replacement immediately. This **preserves** the one-live-link-per-booking invariant rather than relaxing it: the old token is unusable before the new one exists. Contracted on `OtlStatusResponse`.
  - **The fail-closed policy read says why, once per container.** `reportFailClosedPolicy` emits a PHI-free structured `cds_policy_fail_closed` event naming `absent` vs `malformed`, the effect, and the remediation. Once per container per environment, because the read runs on every gated request and an unconditional log would turn a configuration gap into a log-volume incident.
  - **A read-only preflight, and deliberately no auto-seed.** `npm run cds:demo-readiness` reports policy activation, generation mode, canary allowlist validity, ruleset-component presence, and corpus presence, and exits non-zero on a blocker. It **performs no writes.** The originally requested automated seed-on-uninitialized-policy is rejected: activating a bundle server-enables protected clinical generation for **every actor** in an environment with no canary allowlist, and ADR-20260703-01 makes that a governed operation requiring its own point-of-action authorization. A request path that seeded it as a side effect would be the gate authorizing itself.
  - **No corpus fallback was added, because none is needed.** `findPatientEducationForDiagnosis` returns `null` when nothing matches and `protected-generation.ts` already degrades to the generic immutable grounding bundle and deterministic template. An empty corpus produces plainer patient education; it does not produce a `503`. It is graded `warn` in the preflight so nobody chases it as the cause of a denial.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Reject the booking when the client's `scheduledAt` disagrees with the slot's derived instant: rejected. The submitted value is a browser-timezone rendering of a doctor-local string and was never a reliable instant; making it a gate would fail every patient outside UTC+08:00 for no safety gain, since the reservation is what prevents double-booking and the derivation is what fixes the stored time.
  - Anchor the frontend's preselected `Date` to +08:00 instead: rejected for this change set. The picker's option list formats raw wall-clock strings, so anchoring only the preselected value would display a different clock time than the list it came from. Server-side derivation fixes correctness without that inconsistency.
  - Require the client to send the slot's `date` so the slot can be fetched with a single `GetItem`: rejected. It adds a second client-supplied field the server must then validate anyway, to save one bounded query over three dates.
  - Store the timezone on each slot item now: rejected as premature and as a data-model migration. Recorded as the correct fix if a second timezone ever appears.
  - Release a reserved slot inside the cancellation transaction rather than after it: rejected for this change set. The cancel path uses a conditional `UpdateCommand` with `ReturnValues: ALL_NEW` and sequences a refund behind it; restructuring that into a transaction is a larger change than the defect warrants, and a post-commit release conditional on the slot still recording that booking is idempotent and cannot disturb a re-reserved slot.
  - Remove the last two fallbacks in `matchDoctorForBooking` so an unavailable doctor cannot be assigned: rejected as out of scope. It would leave bookings unassigned, which needs an admin-assignment path to land somewhere visible first — a behaviour change, not a defect fix. See trade-offs.
  - Write `chestPain: false` in the no-intake default: rejected outright. It would claim the patient denied chest pain when nobody asked them. The router treats absent and `false` identically, so the two spellings produce the same ROUTINE outcome — but only the omission is true, and only the omission preserves the audit distinction.
  - Leave a consultation with no intake failing closed: rejected. A permanent `503` with no in-application recovery is not the safer alternative; it is an unusable one. The physician conducted the consultation, and the snapshot records that screening was never completed.
  - Void the gateway hold automatically when the payment transaction fails: rejected as not currently expressible. `PaymentGatewayAdapter` has no void operation, and `processRefund` resolves what to refund by loading the payment item that this path never wrote. Recorded as follow-up.
  - Reuse `consumed` for a link that failed to activate: rejected. It would report the link as used when it was not, sending a support conversation in the wrong direction. Mapping it to `expired` would be equally false.
  - Let the issue route supersede any existing active link: rejected. A doctor double-clicking would invalidate a link already sent to the patient. Revoking only links that provably cannot activate is the narrower and safer rule.
  - Auto-seed the CDS policy when the activation record is missing, as originally requested: rejected on safety grounds, stated above.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: a published slot can be reserved by exactly one booking, and the guarantee comes from a transaction rather than from a read-then-write or a client-side check.
  - Positive: slot wall-clock time has one written meaning, and the two places that relate slots to instants now agree with each other by construction.
  - Positive: availability matching does something. Previously it either short-circuited to `true` or never matched, so its result carried no information.
  - Positive: a patient who skips the intake form no longer bricks the physician's post-consult workflow, and the fact that screening never happened is recorded in the immutable snapshot rather than only in a log line.
  - Positive: a payment can no longer be held against a booking that was never confirmed, and an orphaned gateway hold is findable by provider reference instead of by customer complaint.
  - Positive: a doctor whose consultation link fails to activate can issue another one immediately, without the one-live-link rule being weakened.
  - Positive: an operator can tell an unseeded environment from a closed gate, from a log line and from a preflight command, without either being able to open the gate.
  - Trade-offs: slot-backed booking creation costs one extra strongly consistent query, and cancel/reschedule of a slot-backed booking costs a bounded slot walk plus a conditional write. Bookings without `slotId` are unchanged.
  - Trade-offs: `POST /v1/bookings` gains a `404` and a `SLOT_UNAVAILABLE` `409` it did not have. A client that treats any 4xx as a permanent failure will need to distinguish `SLOT_UNAVAILABLE` — the patient must pick another slot, not retry.
  - Trade-offs: **tightening `doctorHasSlotAvailability` does not stop an unavailable doctor being assigned.** The last two fallbacks in `matchDoctorForBooking` return the preferred doctor on KYC approval alone, then simply the first approved doctor. So the fix changes which doctor is *preferred*, not the final outcome when nobody is available. `matcher.test.ts` pins this as a stated property so it is not mistaken for a fix that landed.
  - Trade-offs: a consultation with no patient-reported intake now evaluates ROUTINE and reaches protected generation. That is the deterministic router correctly finding no patient-reported red flag where no patient-reported data exists — not the router being bypassed — but it means the red-flag net contributes nothing on those consultations and the physician is the only safety layer.
  - Trade-offs: the orphaned-hold path is reported, not repaired. Under the default `ledger` provider `createHold` has no external effect, so this is currently a bookkeeping note; under PayRex it would be real money awaiting manual reconciliation.
  - Trade-offs: `revoked` is a new value in the `OtlStatusResponse` status enum. A client with an exhaustive switch on OTL status will not recognise it.
  - Trade-offs: the release path locates a slot by walking the doctor's partition in bounded pages, because the booking does not store the slot's date. A time-ordered slot sort key would remove that walk, and is the same follow-up the overlap guard already wants (ADR-20260806-05).
  - **Not qualified.** Local evidence only: backend typecheck, lint, 947 unit tests, property/integration/handler/legacy-bypass suites, CDS safety regression (`--check`), CDS resilience campaign (261 tests), retirement-manifest check, `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` (17 tests); frontend typecheck, lint, 626 tests, production build, CDS call-manifest check; contract lint and generated-type drift check. **No environment was mutated and no Terraform was applied.** Not staging qualification, not canary authorization or observation, not production enablement. None of the six repairs has been exercised against a deployed API.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) add a void/cancel operation to `PaymentGatewayAdapter` so an orphaned hold can be released automatically instead of reconciled by hand — required before PayRex is enabled; (b) decide whether `matchDoctorForBooking` should return `null` rather than assign an unavailable doctor, which needs an admin-assignment surface first; (c) `backend/src/handlers/on-demand-pool.test.ts` "never exposes patient identity (PBT)" is **flaky** — it generates `patientId` and `chiefComplaint` independently and asserts the id is not a substring of the serialised entry, but `chiefComplaint` flows into `reasonExcerpt`, so a generated id that coincidentally appears inside a generated complaint fails the run. Observed failing once and passing on rerun; it should exclude the generated id from the complaint alphabet; (d) `npm run test:cds-security` executes only one test file (3 passed, 11 skipped) despite listing many — the files themselves all run and pass under `test:unit`, so coverage is intact, but the aggregate gate is not doing what its name claims and should be re-checked; (e) the frontend still preselects and displays slot times in the browser's timezone, which is correct for PH viewers and shifted for others — revisit alongside (f); (f) the time-ordered slot sort key from ADR-20260806-05 would also remove the release-path partition walk.
- Related files: `backend/src/lib/schedule.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/bookings.ts`, `backend/src/__tests__/slot-reservation.test.ts`, `backend/src/lib/matcher.ts`, `backend/src/lib/matcher.test.ts`, `backend/src/lib/payments.ts`, `backend/src/lib/payment-hold-atomicity.test.ts`, `backend/src/lib/otl.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/otl.ts`, `backend/src/lib/otl-recovery.test.ts`, `backend/src/lib/cds/intake-clinical-input.ts`, `backend/src/lib/cds/intake-clinical-input.test.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/payments.ts`, `backend/src/lib/cds/policy.ts`, `backend/src/tools/cds-demo-readiness.ts`, `backend/package.json`, `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `backend/src/contracts/openapi.generated.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/types/openapi.generated.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/createBookingRequest.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/createBookingRequest.slotId.test.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/doctor/DoctorBooking.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/doctor/DoctorBookingLoader.tsx`

### ADR-20260807-02: Do not offer slots that have already started; auth-persistence decision deferred

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: Accepted for the slot filter; **DEFER** on browser session persistence pending an explicit decision
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: Two symptoms were reported from the patient booking flow: reloading the page appears to sign the patient out, and doctor availability is not visible or selectable even after a doctor has published schedules.

  **The reload behaviour is not a defect.** ADR-20260726-01 made browser auth state memory-only. The `bayan-auth` cookie carries only `{ roles, expiresAt }` so `proxy.ts` can make a routing decision; the tokens themselves live in the Zustand store and die with the page. `components/auth/SessionGuard.tsx` already documents exactly this and deliberately redirects to sign-in when the cookie outlives the tokens. `backend/src/__tests__/task10-3-retained-findings.test.ts` guards it with a test named "does not persist auth tokens or intake PHI" (Requirements 12.18, 12.23), asserting `useAuthStore.ts` contains no `zustand/middleware`.

  **The availability symptom has the same cause, and no separate defect was found.** Verified against `dev`: the one KYC-approved doctor (`verificationStatus: approved`, `gsi3pk: KYC#approved`) holds 34 `available` slots dated 2026-08-06/07/08, comfortably inside the 30-day window the client requests; `GET /v1/doctors/{doctorId}/schedules` requires only authentication, with a default page size of 50 so all 34 fit one page; and the detail page renders every available slot. With no in-memory token, `fetchDoctorDetail` throws `AUTH_REQUIRED` before any network call, so the page shows its error state while `SessionGuard` redirects — which presents as "no availability" rather than as a sign-out. **Not verified end to end**: the seeded dev smoke credential is rejected (`NotAuthorizedException`), so no authenticated read was performed against the deployed API. This conclusion rests on data and code inspection only.

  **One genuine defect was found in passing, and it was made user-visible by ADR-20260807-01.** Every surface filtered slots on `status === "available"` alone. That is necessary but not sufficient: a 09:00 slot is still `available` at 15:00 the same day. Since `POST /v1/bookings` now refuses a slot whose start has passed, the patient could select a stale slot and walk the entire booking flow to a `409 SLOT_UNAVAILABLE` at the final step. Sixteen of the dev doctor's 34 slots are dated today, so this was live on the demo path.
- Decision:
  - **A slot is offered only when it is `available` *and* has not started.** `frontend/.../features/booking/lib/slotTime.ts` is the client half of the backend's slot-placement rule: `PLATFORM_UTC_OFFSET_MINUTES` mirrors `backend/src/lib/schedule.ts`, and `slotStartMs` / `isSlotInFuture` / `selectableSlots` place a slot on the timeline the same way the server does rather than using the browser's timezone. Applied at the three places a slot is offered or preselected: the doctor detail page, `mapDoctorToSearchDoctor` (search cards, including `nextAvailable`), and `DoctorBookingLoader`.
  - **An unplaceable slot is treated as not bookable.** A malformed `date`/`startTime` yields `false`, because the server derives the booking instant from those same fields and refuses what it cannot resolve — returning `true` would offer a guaranteed failure.
  - **The server remains the authority.** This filter only avoids presenting a choice that is certain to be refused; it is not the guarantee.
  - **`mapDoctorToSearchDoctor`'s availability projection is now tested.** It had no coverage, which is how a past slot could be advertised unnoticed. `doctors.availability.test.ts` pins the ordering, the exclusions, and `nextAvailable`.
  - **No change to auth persistence. Recorded as `DEFER`.** Making the session survive a reload reverses a Task 10.3 control with a named requirement and a guard test, so it is not taken as an incidental fix. Two options are put to the owner: (1) hold the Cognito refresh token in an `HttpOnly`, `Secure`, `SameSite` cookie and re-mint id/access tokens server-side through a Next route handler on app load — the session survives reload and nothing long-lived is readable by JavaScript, which upholds ADR-20260726-01's intent rather than reversing it; or (2) persist only the id and access tokens to `sessionStorage`, excluding the refresh token, which restores the session for the remainder of the token lifetime and dies with the tab. Option 2 is materially weaker and requires amending ADR-20260726-01 and the guard test.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Implement `sessionStorage` persistence directly as requested: rejected without an explicit decision. Hand-rolling it would pass the guard test's literal assertion — which only looks for `zustand/middleware` — while defeating the purpose the test states in its own name. Passing a security test on a technicality is worse than failing it.
  - Ask the backend for future-only slots instead of filtering client-side: rejected. It adds a query parameter and a contract change to solve a presentation problem, and the server already refuses stale slots at the point that matters.
  - Filter using the browser's local timezone: rejected. That is the defect class ADR-20260807-01 removed from the matcher; the client must use the same stated offset as the server or the two disagree about when a slot begins.
  - Keep offering past slots and explain the `409` in the UI: rejected. The failure arrives after the patient has chosen a service, a channel, and confirmed — the wrong place to discover the slot was never bookable.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: a patient is not offered a slot the server will refuse, so the `409 SLOT_UNAVAILABLE` path introduced by ADR-20260807-01 is not reachable through the normal UI.
  - Positive: search cards no longer advertise a `nextAvailable` date that has already passed.
  - Positive: client and server now share one written rule for placing a slot on the timeline.
  - Trade-offs: a doctor's same-day slots disappear from the patient view as the day progresses, which is correct but means a doctor who publishes only same-day availability will appear to lose it over the day.
  - Trade-offs: the filter runs against the viewer's clock. A device with a badly wrong clock will over- or under-filter; the server's refusal is still authoritative.
  - Trade-offs: **the reported reload behaviour is unchanged.** A patient who reloads mid-booking is still returned to sign-in. `SessionGuard` appends `?next=` and `SignInForm` honours it, so they land back where they were after re-authenticating, but they must re-authenticate.
  - **Not qualified.** Frontend typecheck, lint, and 639 tests pass locally. The dev data and read path were inspected directly; no authenticated request was made against the deployed API, and the reported symptoms were not reproduced end to end.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) the owner decides between the two auth-persistence options above; (b) reproduce the availability symptom with a working dev patient credential to confirm the token diagnosis rather than infer it — the seeded smoke password no longer authenticates and should be reset or documented; (c) consider hiding a doctor from search entirely once they have no future availability, which today shows an empty `scheduleSpace` instead.
- Related files: `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/slotTime.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/slotTime.test.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/api/doctors.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/api/doctors.availability.test.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/app/booking/doctor/[doctorId]/page.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/doctor/DoctorBookingLoader.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/stores/useAuthStore.ts` (unchanged — decision deferred), `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/components/auth/SessionGuard.tsx` (unchanged)

### ADR-20260807-03: Name the cause when a request never completes

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: Accepted
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: "The patient cannot see any doctor available slots" was investigated three times and misdiagnosed twice, because every layer that could be inspected reported healthy and the symptom carried no information.

  What was verified against deployed `dev`, using a temporary synthetic patient created and deleted for the purpose: `GET /v1/doctors` returns the one approved doctor; `GET /v1/doctors/{doctorId}/schedules` returns **38 available slots** for the window the client requests; all 46 slots in the table sit in that doctor's partition and their profile is `verificationStatus: approved`; the frontend's own window calculation and `selectableSlots` filter, replayed against that live response, offer 22 slots; Amplify's build environment carries the correct API base URL and Cognito ids; the deployed CSP `connect-src` includes the API origin; and the Amplify `main` build is current with the latest merge.

  What could not be verified from outside a browser is the only thing left: **API Gateway allows an exact list of two origins** — `https://main.d27420akzuajw.amplifyapp.com` and `http://localhost:3000` — with wildcards forbidden by a Terraform validation rule (ADR-20260726-01). A control request from a disallowed origin returns `200` with **no CORS headers at all**, which a browser blocks. Node ignores CORS entirely, which is exactly why every probe looked clean.

  Serving the app from any other origin — the bare `d27420akzuajw.amplifyapp.com` without the `main.` prefix, a branch subdomain, a custom domain, `127.0.0.1` instead of `localhost`, or a port other than 3000 — therefore fails every API call. **Sign-in still succeeds**, because Cognito is a separate service with its own CORS policy. That asymmetry is what makes a configuration fault present as a data fault: the patient appears signed in, and every screen is simply empty.
- Decision:
  - **A `fetch` rejection becomes a diagnosis, not a bare `TypeError`.** `lib/api.ts` wraps the call and raises `ApiError('NETWORK_UNREACHABLE', …, 0)` naming both the API origin it tried to reach and the page origin it called from, and stating that the usual cause is an origin outside the API's allowed list. Previously `TypeError: Failed to fetch` propagated straight into `AsyncView`'s error slot, so the UI's most common infrastructure failure rendered a message that named neither party.
  - **The original error is preserved as `cause`, not inlined into the message.** The message is rendered to a patient; the underlying failure belongs in the console.
  - **A missing `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_BASE_URL` is reported as itself.** An app built without it has no destination, which is a different fault with a different fix and should not be described as unreachable.
  - **A blocked request does not sign the user out.** `notifyUnauthorized` fires only on an actual `401`. Treating a network failure as an expired session would redirect to sign-in, where signing in appears to succeed and the loop repeats — hiding the cause behind an apparent auth problem.
  - **No origin was added to the allowlist.** The correct entry depends on the URL actually in use, which is not known here, and speculatively widening a security allowlist to make a symptom disappear is the wrong instinct. The owner supplies the origin; the Terraform variable is then changed under its own authorization.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Add the bare Amplify default domain to `frontend_allowed_origins` pre-emptively: rejected. It may not be the origin in use, and it would widen the allowlist on a guess.
  - Relax CORS to a wildcard for `dev`: rejected outright. The Terraform validation forbidding `"*"` exists because of ADR-20260726-01, and a `dev` wildcard would be copied into `staging` by the next person who needed it to work.
  - Detect CORS specifically and say so definitively: not possible. The browser withholds the reason by design, so a CORS denial, a DNS failure, and an offline device are indistinguishable to JavaScript. The message names the likely cause without asserting it.
  - Retry a network failure automatically: rejected. A blocked origin fails identically on every attempt, and retrying would delay the error the reader needs.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the next occurrence of this class of fault states both origins in the UI, which is enough to identify it without a code investigation.
  - Positive: an origin misconfiguration can no longer masquerade as an auth failure, because it no longer triggers the sign-out path.
  - Trade-offs: the message is longer and more technical than a patient-facing error should be. Accepted for now — this is a pre-production diagnostic aid, and a fault that blocks every request is not a state a patient should reach in production. It should be shortened once the environment matrix is stable.
  - Trade-offs: the diagnosis is a likelihood, not a determination. It will name allowed origins as the probable cause even when the real fault is a dropped connection.
  - **Not qualified.** Frontend typecheck, lint, and 644 tests pass. The underlying report is **not resolved** — the fix here makes the fault legible, it does not remove it. The root cause remains unconfirmed pending the origin actually in use.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) obtain the exact URL in the address bar and, if it is outside the allowed list, add it to `frontend_allowed_origins` for `dev` under its own authorization and re-apply; (b) if the origin is already allowed, the next failure will carry the real message and the investigation restarts from that; (c) the seeded dev smoke credential no longer authenticates, which blocked reproduction twice — reset or document it; (d) consider a startup check that logs the configured API origin so a mismatch is visible before a user hits it.
- Related files: `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/lib/api.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/lib/api.network.test.ts`, `infra/environments/dev/variables.tf` (unchanged — `frontend_allowed_origins`), `infra/modules/http_api/main.tf` (unchanged)

### ADR-20260807-04: A picker that asks for a time must be given the times

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: Accepted
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: The reported symptom — "the patient cannot see any doctor available slots" — was in the **intake form**, not on the doctor detail page, and had nothing to do with slots, auth, or CORS. A screenshot of the failing dialog identified it in one step after three rounds of investigation had looked in the wrong place.

  `AdditionalInfoSection`'s "Consultation Date and Time" field renders `DateTimePicker` and stores a date only: `onDateChange` formats with `format(date, "yyyy-MM-dd")`, the schema is `dateOfConsultation: z.string()`, and the review step renders it with `toLocaleDateString`. The time is discarded at every step.

  But `DateTimePicker`'s `enableTime` defaults to `true`. So after choosing a day the dialog advanced to a time step; that step lists `availableTimes`, which this caller — having no doctor in scope — did not pass; and `Confirm` is `disabled={!hasSelectedTime}`. The patient reached a panel reading "This doctor has no published times. Pick another doctor, or request a consultation without a preferred time." above a permanently greyed-out `Confirm`, **for every date**. The intake form could not be completed.

  Two earlier and individually correct changes combined to produce it. Removing the fabricated `["09:00 AM" … "11:30 AM"]` fallback was right — it had let patients pick times no doctor had published. But that fallback had been masking a wrong default in this caller, so removing it converted invented data into a dead end. And the empty-state copy asserted a doctor, which is simply untrue on a form that has none.
- Decision:
  - **The intake field collects a date.** `enableTime={false}` on the only caller that stores a date. `handleDateSelect` then commits and closes, with no time step to be stranded in.
  - **Requiring a time without supplying times is no longer expressible.** `DateTimePickerProps` becomes a discriminated union: `{ enableTime: false; availableTimes?: never }` or `{ enableTime?: true; availableTimes: string[] }`. Because `enableTime` defaults to `true`, a caller that passes neither now fails to compile — which is exactly the mistake that shipped. This immediately caught two further call sites in `DateTimePicker.test.tsx` that had the same shape.
  - **The empty-times copy no longer mentions a doctor by default.** `noTimesMessage` defaults to "No times are available to choose from for this date." `DoctorBooking` passes the doctor-specific wording, because a doctor genuinely is in scope there.
  - **The regression tests assert after a day is selected, not before.** `showCalendar` starts `true` either way, so asserting on the freshly-opened dialog would pass under both behaviours and prove nothing. Reverting `enableTime={false}` fails three of the four.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Pass the doctor's real slots into the intake field: rejected. The intake form is not scoped to a doctor — it captures a *preferred* consultation date before any doctor is chosen. Slot selection belongs on the doctor detail page, which already does it.
  - Have `DateTimePicker` silently drop the time requirement when no times are supplied: rejected. It would repair this caller by making the component's contract ambiguous, and a doctor-scoped picker that genuinely has no times for a date must still say so rather than quietly accepting a date.
  - Keep `enableTime` optional and add a runtime warning: rejected. A `console.warn` in a component that renders fine in tests is exactly how this survived; the type makes it unshippable instead.
  - Only fix the copy: rejected. The copy was misleading, but the blocker was the disabled `Confirm`.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the intake form can be completed. This was a hard blocker on the patient booking path.
  - Positive: the same mistake cannot be made again without a compile error, in a component with several callers and a default that invites it.
  - Positive: a form with no doctor in scope no longer tells the patient to "pick another doctor".
  - Trade-offs: the prop union is more complex to read than two independent optional props, and a caller that wants a time must now pass `availableTimes={[]}` explicitly to express "a time is required but none are available". That verbosity is the point.
  - Trade-offs: the intake field no longer captures a preferred *time*, only a date. It never stored one, so nothing is lost — but if a preferred time is wanted later it must be added deliberately, with real options behind it.
  - **Not qualified.** Frontend typecheck, lint (0 errors), and 648 tests pass locally, and the regression tests were confirmed to fail against the previous behaviour. Not verified against the deployed environment by a human walking the flow.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) walk the full patient booking flow on the deployed dev app to confirm the form now submits; (b) three rounds of investigation went to the wrong layer because the report did not identify the screen — a screenshot resolved it immediately, which is worth remembering; (c) the `DateTimePicker` empty state is still reachable on the doctor detail page when a doctor has no future slots for the chosen date, which is correct but worth confirming reads well.
- Related files: `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/DateTimePicker.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/DateTimePicker.test.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/consultation/intake/AdditionalInfoSection.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/consultation/intake/AdditionalInfoSection.dateOnly.test.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/doctor/DoctorBooking.tsx`

### ADR-20260807-05: Reload-surviving sessions via an HttpOnly refresh cookie; a preferred time on the intake form

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally; not qualified in any environment. Amends ADR-20260726-01.
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: Two requests, plus one finding neither asked for.

  **Reloading signed the user out.** ADR-20260726-01 made browser auth state memory-only: tokens lived in the Zustand store, the `bayan-auth` cookie carried only `{ roles, expiresAt }` for `proxy.ts`, and `SessionGuard` deliberately redirected to sign-in when it saw the cookie outlive the tokens. `task10-3-retained-findings.test.ts` guards it with a test named "does not persist auth tokens or intake PHI" (Requirements 12.18, 12.23). The control was correct about what it forbade — a renewable credential in `localStorage` is an XSS-exfiltratable account takeover — but it treated "do not persist tokens where JavaScript can read them" and "end the session on reload" as the same thing. They are not.

  **The intake form's "Consultation Date and Time" field collected only a date.** The legend promised a time; the control delivered a date. This is the same field that was a hard dead end until ADR-20260807-04.

  **And that field is never transmitted.** `intakeMapper.ts` maps only `additionalInfo.additionalConcerns` and `additionalInfo.consent`. `dateOfConsultation` is collected, validated as required, and rendered back to the patient — and then dropped. It reaches no endpoint and no DynamoDB item.
- Decision:
  - **The refresh token moves to an `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax`, path-scoped cookie, and never returns to the browser.** `POST /api/auth/session` takes custody of it once at sign-in; `POST /api/auth/refresh` exchanges it server-side for fresh id/access tokens; `DELETE /api/auth/session` clears it on sign-out. `Secure` is set whenever the request is HTTPS, read from the URL or `x-forwarded-proto` because Amplify terminates TLS upstream.
  - **Id and access tokens remain memory-only.** They are returned in the refresh response body because every API call sends them, and they are short-lived, which is exactly why that is acceptable and why the refresh token is not. `restoreSession` stores an empty `refreshToken`, so no client code can refresh.
  - **This upholds ADR-20260726-01 rather than reversing it.** No token is readable by JavaScript, no `zustand/middleware`, no `localStorage`, no `sessionStorage` — the guard test passes on its merits, not on a technicality. `sessionStorage` was rejected for exactly that reason: it would have passed the literal assertion (which greps for `zustand/middleware`) while defeating the purpose the test states in its own name.
  - **Session state gains an origin.** `SessionOrigin` is `unknown | restoring | signed-in | restored | none`, and `SessionGuard` may only redirect once it is resolved. On a fresh load the store is empty, so "not authenticated" is an unfinished question, not a fact — redirecting during that window is what made a reload look like a sign-out, and it would still do so with restoration in place. The guard subscribes to the store rather than reading once, because the answer arrives after its effect runs.
  - **A failed refresh is not the same as a dead one.** `401` clears the cookie and resolves to `none`. A network failure or `503` resolves without clearing anything: the outcome is unknown, and discarding a possibly-valid session on a transient failure is the bug this distinction avoids. A build with no `NEXT_PUBLIC_COGNITO_CLIENT_ID` reports `AUTH_NOT_CONFIGURED` (500) rather than masquerading as an expired session.
  - **Sign-in and sign-out fail closed around refresh-cookie custody.** A newly authenticated identity is not installed in memory until `POST /api/auth/session` confirms that its refresh token replaced any prior cookie. `DELETE /api/auth/session` failure is propagated so sign-out retains the current in-memory session instead of claiming success while a renewable credential remains. This prevents reload from silently restoring a different prior account after an apparently successful account switch.
  - **Cookie lifetime is capped at 12 hours**, well below Cognito's 30-day refresh-token default, so an abandoned browser cannot resume a clinical session for a month.
  - **The intake field collects a date and an optional time, via a free time input — not a list.** A list of clickable times reads as "these are bookable", which this form cannot promise: it has no doctor in scope and no schedule. Supplying a generic list would be the fabricated-availability pattern ADR-20260806-02 removed, in a new costume. A free input asks for a preference and claims nothing, and the copy now says so: "This is a preference, not a confirmed appointment — your consultation time is set by the slot you book with a doctor."
  - **A time cannot exist without a date.** The input is disabled until a date is chosen and `withTimeSegment` refuses to produce one, because a bare time is not actionable and would render as an unparseable value in the review step.
  - **The review step renders both halves in local terms**, built field-by-field. Handing a date-only string to `new Date` parses it as UTC midnight, which renders as the previous day for any viewer west of UTC.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Persist the store to `sessionStorage`, excluding the refresh token: rejected. Smaller than `localStorage` but still hands bearer tokens to any injected script, and it defeats a guard test's stated purpose while passing its letter.
  - Move sign-in server-side so the refresh token never touches the browser at all: the correct end state, and **not done here**. `lib/cognito.ts` authenticates against `cognito-idp` directly from the browser, so the token crosses the wire once into `POST /api/auth/session`. Recorded as follow-up rather than smuggled into this change.
  - Call Cognito `GlobalSignOut` on sign-out: rejected. It revokes the user's other sessions too, which is not what a sign-out button on one device should mean. Revoking this specific refresh token is a follow-up.
  - `SameSite=Strict`: rejected. The cookie must survive a top-level navigation back into the app, such as an emailed consultation link.
  - Give the intake time field a generic list of clinic hours: rejected, per the fabricated-availability reasoning above.
  - Wire `dateOfConsultation` to the backend as part of this change: rejected as scope. There is no contract field for a preferred time, and adding one is a contract-first change with its own design question — whether a preference should exist at all once a slot is booked.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: a reload, a refresh, and a bookmarked deep link all keep the user signed in for up to 12 hours, without a renewable credential being reachable from JavaScript.
  - Positive: an XSS is bounded to the current session's token lifetime instead of the refresh token's, which is a materially smaller blast radius than any client-storage option.
  - Positive: the guard can no longer redirect on an unanswered question, which removes a whole class of "it logged me out" reports.
  - Positive: the intake field's control finally matches its label.
  - Trade-offs: the refresh token crosses the wire once, in a request body, because sign-in is still client-side. Stated rather than hidden; the fix is the server-side sign-in follow-up.
  - Trade-offs: an XSS can still *call* `/api/auth/refresh` from the victim's browser and obtain a fresh short-lived token. Cookie custody prevents exfiltration, not use.
  - Trade-offs: two new server routes mean the app now depends on the Next.js runtime for auth. It already did for `proxy.ts`, so this adds no new deployment requirement, but a static export would no longer work.
  - Trade-offs: first paint on a reload now waits for one refresh round trip before the guard decides. That replaces an instant wrong answer with a brief correct one.
  - Trade-offs: the preferred time still goes nowhere. The patient is now told that plainly, which is better than implying otherwise, but it remains a field that collects data the platform discards.
  - **Not qualified.** Frontend typecheck, lint (0 errors), 660 tests; the Task 10.3 guard suite (19 tests) re-run and passing. No environment mutated. Not verified by a human reloading the deployed app.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) move sign-in server-side so the refresh token never reaches the browser; (b) revoke the specific refresh token on sign-out; (c) decide whether `dateOfConsultation` should be contracted and transmitted, or removed in favour of the booked slot; (d) `SessionOrigin` now exists but no UI distinguishes `restored` from `signed-in` — if a "welcome back" affordance is ever wanted, that is where it belongs.
- Related files: `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/app/api/auth/session/route.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/app/api/auth/refresh/route.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/app/api/auth/cookie.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/lib/session-restore.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/stores/useAuthStore.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/components/auth/SessionGuard.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/authentication/components/forms/SignInForm.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/hooks/use-sign-out.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/consultation/intake/AdditionalInfoSection.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/consultation/intake/dateOfConsultation.test.ts`

### ADR-20260807-06: A manually provisioned video link as an explicit stand-in for the gated Meet integration

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: Accepted for `dev` only — demo stand-in, not the ADR-20260720-02 integration
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: The demo needs a video affordance. ADR-20260720-02 accepted Google Meet as the MVP video transport and then gated implementation on decisions that remain unmade: the organizer identity model (platform-owned Workspace identity vs per-doctor OAuth), Workspace plan validation, data-processing terms, retention, and patient/doctor notice. It also requires join URIs to come from the provider's `spaces.create` API and never be constructed locally. There are no Google credentials in this AWS account, so the real integration cannot be built today.

  The alternative to a stand-in was fabricating a plausible link. A dead `meet.google.com/...` URL discovered by a doctor and a patient mid-consultation is worse than an absent button, because the participants act on it.
- Decision:
  - **One operator-supplied link per environment**, in `DEMO_VIDEO_JOIN_URL`, defaulted for `dev` to the space the owner created by hand and empty everywhere else. Set in `infra/environments/dev/variables.tf` rather than in the module default so staging and prod stay empty unless someone deliberately changes them.
  - **The stand-in's limits are recorded in the code, the variable description, and the contract**, because they are the kind of thing that gets forgotten: it is one room for the whole environment (two concurrent consultations would meet each other in it), it has no lifecycle, and its access control is Google's rather than ours.
  - **The ADR's disclosure rules are honoured even though the link is not real.** Returned only by `GET /v1/bookings/{bookingId}`, only to the owning patient or assigned doctor, only while the booking is `confirmed` or `in_progress`. Attached in the handler rather than in `toApiBooking`, because that mapper also projects the list endpoints and the join URI must not appear there. Never logged.
  - **`confirmed` is eligible, not just `in_progress`.** A link that appears only once the doctor has started leaves the patient unable to be in the room when they arrive.
  - **A malformed value resolves to "not configured", never passed through.** Validated against the documented Meet space shape in both Terraform and the Lambda, so a typo or an injected value cannot become a link the product hands to a patient.
  - **The frontend opens it in a new context with `rel="noopener noreferrer"`** and does not render the URL as text. `noopener` denies the opened page a handle on the app window; `noreferrer` keeps the booking URL, which carries a booking id, out of the referrer sent to Google.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Construct a plausible Meet URL: rejected. It violates the ADR explicitly and produces a dead link at the worst moment.
  - Store the link per booking in DynamoDB: rejected for now. It implies per-consultation provisioning that does not exist, and would outlive the stand-in as a data-model artifact. Environment configuration is honest about being one shared room and is deleted in one line.
  - Hardcode the link in application code: rejected. It would ship to staging and prod with the artifact.
  - Wait for the real integration: rejected as the only option that leaves the demo with no video at all.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: both participants get a working video button today, with the ADR's privacy rules already in force so the real integration inherits them.
  - Positive: staging and prod are unaffected and will show no video button.
  - Trade-offs: **it is one shared room.** Fine for a single scripted demo, unacceptable with real patients. This is the reason the value is empty by default.
  - Trade-offs: nothing ends the meeting or revokes access, and anyone holding the link can join.
  - Trade-offs: a `dev` Terraform apply is required for the Lambda to see the variable.
  - **Not qualified.** Backend typecheck, lint, 964 tests (17 new); frontend typecheck, lint, 660 tests; contract lint and generated-type drift; `terraform validate` and `fmt -check` across all three environments. Not applied to `dev` and not exercised against the deployed API.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) apply `dev` so the Lambda receives `DEMO_VIDEO_JOIN_URL`; (b) replace this module with the three contracted `video-session` routes once the organizer identity model and privacy gates are settled — `lib/video-session.ts` is deliberately small so that change deletes it; (c) the doctor surface does not yet show the link, only the patient booking detail does; (d) OTL still owns the `confirmed -> in_progress` transition and the CDS canonical session, so video does not yet replace it.
- Related files: `backend/src/lib/video-session.ts`, `backend/src/lib/video-session.test.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/bookings.ts`, `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `infra/modules/http_api/main.tf`, `infra/modules/http_api/variables.tf`, `infra/environments/dev/main.tf`, `infra/environments/dev/variables.tf`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/api/bookingDetail.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/patient/PatientBookingDetail.tsx`

### ADR-20260808-01: Direct consultation start, replacing the OTL's state transition

- Date: 2026-08-08
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally; not qualified in any environment
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: The owner asked for the one-time link to be replaced by Google Meet. Meet replaces *joining*; it does not replace what the OTL actually did. OTL activation was the only writer of the `confirmed -> in_progress` transition, and that transition does two things the rest of the system depends on: it unlocks chat (`lib/chat-access.ts` requires `in_progress` **and** an active session) and it creates the canonical CDS session that `POST /v1/bookings/{id}/complete` later ends to initialize the Assessment aggregates. Deleting the OTL first would have left nothing able to start a consultation at all.

  The doctor flow was also already routing around the OTL's design. `useStartConsultation` minted a link and immediately consumed it in the same function, because the doctor needed the transition and the token was in the way. A transient failure between those two calls left an unconsumed link whose token nobody could re-read — the exact lockout ADR-20260807-02 had to add `revokeOtl` to recover from.
- Decision:
  - **`POST /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/start`**, assigned doctor only, consult-approved KYC, `Idempotency-Key` required. Same authorization as issuing an OTL, minus the token.
  - **The transaction is deliberately the same shape as `consumeOtlAndCreateSession` minus the OTL update** — same canonical session items, same conditional booking update on `#status = :confirmed AND patientId AND doctorId`. The state a consultation reaches must be identical however it was started, because everything downstream was built against the OTL's output.
  - **An already-active session is joined, not rejected**: `200` with the existing session, `201` when one was created. A doctor double-clicking Start lands in the room. Exactly-once creation is still enforced by the transaction, not by the pre-read — the canonical items use `attribute_not_exists` and the booking update is conditional, so a concurrent second start fails rather than producing two sessions.
  - **`in_progress` with no active session is a state conflict, not an invitation to create one.** Nothing should produce it, and silently attaching a second session to a booking already past `confirmed` would be worse than refusing.
  - **Not-found and not-assigned collapse to one `404`**, so a doctor cannot probe for bookings that are not theirs.
  - **Id generation moved to `lib/consultation-ids.ts`** and is shared. `consultationId` keys every CDS aggregate a consultation produces, so two generators drifting apart would be a data-model divergence rather than a cosmetic one.
  - **The OTL routes stay for now.** They are the path for handing an unauthenticated link to a recipient, and retiring them is a separate change once no frontend caller remains. The frontend no longer calls them.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Have the Meet link itself start the consultation: rejected. The link is a Google URL the platform does not observe; nothing about opening it is visible to the backend, so it cannot drive a state transition or create a CDS session.
  - Delete the OTL now and add the start route in the same change: rejected as two risks in one step. The start path should be proven before the only alternative is removed.
  - Keep issue-then-activate and just wrap it: rejected. It preserves the failure window between two writes that produced the original lockout.
  - Serve the route from the `bookings` Lambda: rejected for now. The canonical-session machinery, the KYC gate, and the audit helper all already live in the OTL handler; moving them is churn that the OTL retirement will revisit anyway.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: a doctor starts a consultation in one authenticated call, with no token that can be stranded and no window between two writes.
  - Positive: the OTL can now be retired without leaving consultations unstartable.
  - Positive: chat unlock and the CDS session are reached identically to before, so the assessment-first chain is unaffected.
  - Trade-offs: two routes now perform the same transition. Intentional and temporary, but until the OTL is retired there are two paths to keep in agreement.
  - Trade-offs: the route lives in a handler named `otl.ts`, which will read oddly until that retirement.
  - Trade-offs: `201` vs `200` is the only signal distinguishing "created" from "joined". A caller that ignores the status code cannot tell.
  - **Not qualified.** Backend typecheck, lint, 977 tests (13 new); frontend typecheck, lint, 660 tests; contract lint and generated-type drift; `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` (17 tests, route/contract parity); `terraform fmt`. The route is defined in Terraform but **not applied**, so it does not exist on the deployed API until `Deploy Dev` runs.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) retire `POST /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/otl`, `GET /v1/otl/{token}`, `POST /v1/otl/{token}/activate`, the `revoked` status, and `/consultation/[token]` once this is exercised in dev; (b) move the route to the `bookings` Lambda as part of that; (c) the patient still has no start affordance, by design — only the assigned doctor may start.
- Related files: `backend/src/lib/consultation-start.ts`, `backend/src/lib/consultation-start.test.ts`, `backend/src/lib/consultation-ids.ts`, `backend/src/lib/otl.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/otl.ts`, `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `infra/modules/http_api/main.tf`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/lib/api/consultSession.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/hooks/useStartConsultation.ts`

### ADR-20260808-02: Disclose the patient's name in the request pool before acceptance

- Date: 2026-08-08
- Status: Accepted — implemented and applied to `dev`; not qualified in any environment. **Supersedes ADR-20260805-04 on identity disclosure only**; every other part of that ADR stands.
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: ADR-20260805-04 built the on-demand request pool to withhold every identifying field until a doctor accepted. A pool entry answered only "what kind of consultation, when, for how much, and roughly why". The reasoning was that a doctor browsing unclaimed requests is not yet a treating clinician for those patients, so they should not learn who they are.

  The owner overrode this on product grounds: a doctor deciding whether to accept should see who they would be treating, the same way a ride-hailing driver sees a rider's name before accepting a trip. Withholding it made the pool feel like a queue of tickets rather than a queue of people, and gave the doctor nothing to greet the patient with when the room opened.

  This ADR is being recorded late. The change shipped in PR #30 and five source files already cite `ADR-20260808-02`, but the entry itself was never written into this log — so the codebase referenced a decision that could not be read. That gap is the reason this entry exists at all, and it is worth naming: a dangling ADR reference is worse than no reference, because it looks like governance while carrying none.
- Decision:
