  - **`OnDemandPoolEntry.patientName` is disclosed pre-acceptance**, resolved through `resolvePatientDisplayName` from the `patient_profile` entity added in the preceding slice.
  - **Absent, never a placeholder, and never the email.** A patient who has saved no profile yields no `patientName`, and the client falls back to the booking reference — which is what the platform actually knows about them. Substituting an email would disclose a contact channel this decision does not grant.
  - **Everything else stays withheld**: no `patientId`, no date of birth, no contact details, and no full intake body — only the bounded `POOL_REASON_MAX` chief-complaint excerpt.
  - **One profile read per pooled request**, alongside the intake read the pool already performed, and it degrades to `null` on failure. A profile problem must not drop a paid request out of the pool.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Keep withholding until acceptance: rejected by the owner. This was the prior decision and it lost on product grounds, not on technical ones.
  - Disclose initials or a first name only: rejected. It buys very little privacy — the excerpt and the accept action already reveal far more — while making the pool read as evasive.
  - Disclose the name only once intake is submitted: rejected. It couples an identity decision to an unrelated form state, and a patient who has paid but not finished intake is exactly the case a doctor most needs context on.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: a doctor accepts a named person rather than a ticket, and can open the room already knowing who they are speaking to.
  - Positive: no new key, index, or entity — the name is read from the profile item the previous slice introduced.
  - **Trade-off, stated plainly: a doctor browsing the pool now learns the names of patients they never treat.** That is a real disclosure widening. It is accepted because the pool is readable only by consult-approved doctors, is scoped to unclaimed requests, and a clinician choosing whether to take a case has a defensible reason to know whose case it is. It is not accepted as a precedent for widening any other pre-acceptance field.
  - Trade-offs: one extra DynamoDB read per pooled request per pool refresh. Bounded by page size and cheap, but it grows with pool depth.
  - **Not qualified.** Enabled in `dev` only (`ON_DEMAND_POOL_ENABLED = true` set in the dev environment, not as a module default). Enabling the pool also stops auto-assignment of on-demand bookings in that environment.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) `backend/src/handlers/on-demand-pool.test.ts` "never exposes patient identity (PBT)" is flaky for the reason recorded under ADR-20260807-01 follow-up (c) and now also asserts against a field this ADR deliberately discloses — it needs rewriting against the current disclosure set, not just de-flaking; (b) if the pool is ever opened beyond consult-approved doctors, this decision must be revisited before that happens.
- Related files: `backend/src/lib/on-demand-pool.ts`, `backend/src/lib/patient-profile.ts`, `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/components/RequestPool.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/lib/api/requestPool.ts`, `infra/environments/dev/main.tf`, `infra/environments/dev/variables.tf`

### ADR-20260808-03: A truthful on-demand waiting screen with a pre-acceptance exit

- Date: 2026-08-08
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally; not qualified in any environment
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: With the request pool enabled (ADR-20260805-04, ADR-20260808-02), a patient can now pay for an on-demand consultation and land in a state the platform had no screen for. The booking sits `confirmed` with no `doctorId`, the wizard routes it to the finding step, and the finding step rendered a progress bar parked at 66% under "Matching you with an available doctor…".

  Three things were wrong with that, in increasing order of seriousness.

  **The copy was false.** No matcher runs for a pooled request. `isOnDemandPoolEnabled()` bypasses auto-assignment precisely so the request waits for a human to claim it via `claimOnDemandRequest`. The screen described server-side matching that was not happening.

  **The mode was being guessed.** `toWizardBooking` derived `bookingType: detail.doctorId ? "regular" : "on-demand"`, so *every* unassigned booking was labelled on-demand — a scheduled booking awaiting the matcher got on-demand treatment, and an accepted on-demand booking stopped being one. `bookingMode` was already on the record and in the contract; the client simply was not reading it.

  **There was no way out.** A paid, unclaimed request has a live payment hold against it and no doctor obliged to appear. The patient's only options were to keep watching the bar or abandon the tab. The backend has supported cancel-with-refund the whole time (`PUT /v1/bookings/{id}` with `status: cancelled` refunds a `held` payment when the prior status was `confirmed`), and it releases the reserved slot in the same request. None of it was reachable from the screen where it was needed.
- Decision:
  - **Read `bookingMode` from the record.** `BookingDetail.bookingMode` is surfaced and `toWizardBooking` maps it directly. The mode is never inferred from the absence of an assignee.
  - **Mode-specific polling copy.** `useFinding` renders "Waiting for a doctor to accept your request…" for `on_demand` and keeps "Matching you with an available doctor…" for `scheduled`. The two are waiting on different things and must not share a sentence.
  - **An on-demand waiting panel composed *onto* the polling view, not substituted for it.** The live badge, spinner, and progress bar are correct for both modes — the poll genuinely is live — so the panel adds to them rather than replacing them. This also kept the existing finding-step tests meaningful instead of rewriting them to accommodate a new branch.
  - **Elapsed wait, and no estimate.** The panel shows how long the patient has waited, ticking each second, derived from the booking's own `updatedAt` — which for an unclaimed pooled request is the payment hold that put it there, since nothing else writes to it while it waits. **No estimated remaining time is shown at any point.** There is no queue depth, no acceptance-rate history, and no signal about whether a doctor is about to look at the pool, so any figure would be fabricated. `formatWaitElapsed` is pure and unit-tested, and `isUsableWaitStart` rejects the epoch so the `new Date(0)` fallback in `toWizardBooking` cannot render as a 56-year wait.
  - **State what is actually happening**, in the terms the backend implements: a broadcast to verified doctors, claimed atomically by the first to accept.
  - **State that the money is held, not taken**, and that cancelling now releases it in full — because that is exactly what the server does.
  - **The cancel affordance lives on the waiting panel only, not as a general control on the booking page.** Pre-acceptance is the one point where a full release is settled policy. After a doctor accepts, the intended policy is a two-minute grace window and then a partial capture, and the backend does not implement that yet — so a general cancel button would quietly grant full refunds in cases that are not meant to receive them. Scoping it here keeps the UI from outrunning the payment rules.
  - **One idempotency key per mounted cancel action, held in a ref — not one per attempt.** `useCancelBooking` generates the key once so a retry after an ambiguous network failure replays the same write and receives the stored response. With a fresh key per attempt, the second write would find the booking already `cancelled`, fail the status condition, and report `409` — telling the patient their cancellation failed while their money was already being released. This is the whole reason the key is not left to the transport's auto-generation.
  - **Nothing is patched locally on success.** Cancellation has server-side effects beyond the status field (slot release, refund), so the booking is re-read.
  - **A `409` gets its own message.** "This booking has already moved on" is actionable; a raw conflict string is not. A failed cancellation leaves the waiting panel in place, because the request is in fact still pending.
  - **Distinct labels on the confirmation dialog.** The trigger reads "Cancel request" and the confirming action reads "Yes, withdraw it". "Cancel" beside "Cancel" is the classic confirm-dialog trap where the destructive and the dismissive option are indistinguishable.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Show an estimated wait or a countdown: rejected. The platform holds nothing to compute one from, and a wrong estimate on a paid clinical request is worse than no estimate.
  - Replace the progress bar with a bespoke on-demand view: rejected. The poll is live and the staged progress is honest for both modes; substituting a branch would have discarded working, tested UI to no benefit.
  - Add a general "Cancel booking" control to the booking detail page: rejected for now, as above — the post-acceptance payment rules do not exist yet, so the control would have the wrong consequence in every case except this one.
  - Auto-expire the pooled request after ten minutes and release the hold: **deferred, not rejected.** It is the right behaviour and is the agreed policy, but it needs a backend sweeper or on-read expiry and belongs with the no-show and grace-window work, not with a screen.
  - Add a dedicated `pooledAt` timestamp: rejected as premature. `updatedAt` is exact for an unclaimed request, and the code says so rather than pretending otherwise.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the screen now describes what the system is actually doing, and the patient can leave a request nobody has taken without losing money.
  - Positive: reading `bookingMode` fixes a mislabelling that affected scheduled bookings too, which had nothing to do with the on-demand feature.
  - Positive: cancelling releases the slot and refunds the hold through the existing, already-tested backend path — no new server behaviour was added for this slice.
  - Trade-offs: `updatedAt` stands in for a pool-entry timestamp. Exact today because nothing else writes to an unclaimed pooled booking; it would silently become wrong if anything else began to.
  - Trade-offs: a one-second interval re-renders the panel while it is mounted. Confined to one small subtree on one step.
  - Trade-offs: **an unclaimed request still waits forever unless the patient cancels.** This slice gives them the exit; it does not add the expiry. Until the deferred expiry lands, a patient who closes the tab keeps a hold open indefinitely.
  - **Not qualified.** Frontend typecheck clean, lint 0 errors (52 pre-existing warnings, none new), 688 tests across 99 files (14 new). No backend, contract, or Terraform change — `bookingMode` and `UpdateBookingRequest.status: cancelled` were already contracted, so this slice adds no route and no schema.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) implement the ten-minute pool expiry with a full hold release; (b) implement the post-acceptance cancellation policy (two-minute grace, then 50% captured with the doctor receiving the normal 80% share, no refund once `in_progress`) and only then consider a general cancel control; (c) the no-show endpoint applying the same split after a ten-minute mandatory wait; (d) the patient still sees "No gender preference • Any language" on the finding step because `toWizardBooking` supplies empty preference arrays the backend never returns — pre-existing noise, worth removing when preferences are actually plumbed through.
- Related files: `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/waitElapsed.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/waitElapsed.test.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/api/cancelBooking.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/api/bookingDetail.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/hooks/useCancelBooking.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/hooks/useFinding.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/consultation/FindingStep.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/consultation/FindingStep.onDemand.test.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/patient/PatientBookingDetail.tsx`

### ADR-20260808-04: Retire the one-time link entirely

- Date: 2026-08-08
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally; the Terraform removal is not applied until `Deploy Dev` runs. **Completes the follow-up actions of ADR-20260808-01.** Supersedes the OTL portions of ADR-20260807-01 (the `revoked` status) and ADR-20260805-01 remains untouched — the *intake* link is a separate surviving feature.
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A) — direction from the owner ("deleted entirely")
- Context: ADR-20260808-01 added `POST /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/start` and deliberately left the OTL routes in place, on the grounds that the start path should be proven before the only alternative was removed. It has since been applied to `dev` and exercised. The owner's instruction is to delete the OTL outright, with Google Meet covering *joining* and `/start` covering the state transition.

  The OTL was never really a link feature; it was the only writer of the `confirmed -> in_progress` transition, and that transition unlocks chat (`lib/chat-access.ts` requires `in_progress` **and** an active session) and creates the canonical CDS session that `POST /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/complete` later ends. That is why it could not simply be deleted when Meet arrived.

  It also had a genuine defect that no amount of patching removed: single-use tokens meant whoever opened the link consumed it and locked the other participant out of the token-scoped page. `useStartConsultation` had already routed around the whole design by minting a link and consuming it in the same function, which left a window where a transient failure stranded a live token nobody could read — the lockout `revokeOtl` was added to recover from in ADR-20260807-01.
- Decision:
  - **Relocate before deleting, in a separate applied change set.** `/start` was served *by the OTL Lambda*: the Terraform route targeted `aws_apigatewayv2_integration.otl` and `handleStartConsultation` lived in `handlers/otl.ts`. A single change set would have deleted the only way to start a consultation. So the relocation shipped on its own, was applied by `Deploy Dev`, and was verified against live `dev` — the route now maps to `bayanhealth-dev-bookings`, and a direct Lambda invoke with that `routeKey` returns `AUTH_TOKEN_INVALID` from `requireAuth` rather than "Route not found", which proves dispatch rather than merely wiring. Only then was any deletion performed.
  - **`SessionItem` and `toPublicSession` move to `lib/sessions.ts`.** They lived in `lib/otl.ts` because the OTL was once the only thing that created a session. Four modules imported the type through the OTL module by historical accident, and `handlers/chat.ts` reached in for the projector to serve `GET /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/state`.
  - **Delete**: `handlers/otl.ts`, `lib/otl.ts`, `lib/otl-recovery.test.ts`; the three contract paths (`issueOtl`, `inspectOtl`, `activateOtl`) and the four OTL-only components (`OtlTokenPath`, `OtlIssueResponse`, `OtlStatusResponse`, `OtlActivationResponse`); the `package:lambda-otl` script and its `package-lambdas.mjs` entry; the Lambda, log group, invoke permission, integration, and three routes in Terraform, plus the module variables/outputs and the per-environment locals/arguments/outputs in all three environments; and on the frontend `app/consultation/[token]/`, `ConsultationSessionView`, `useOtlActivation`, `otlActivation.ts`, and their tests.
  - **The `revoked` status goes with it.** It existed only to recover from the stranded-token lockout, which cannot occur without tokens.
  - **`KeyPrefix.Otl`, `EntityType.Otl`, its `SchemaVersion`, and `Ttl.Otl` are kept and marked retired-but-reserved; only the key builders (`otlPrimaryKey`, `otlBookingIndexKey`) are deleted.** This is the one place the deletion is deliberately incomplete, and the reason is data rather than code: `OTL#` is burned into items that may still sit in a table or a backup, and reusing the prefix for a future entity would collide with them. Reserved is the honest state — retired, not free.
  - **`lib/cds/legacy-classifier.ts` keeps its `otl` kind.** It classifies pre-existing OTL records for the CDS migration, and `CLASSIFIER_VERSION` hashes `RULE_SET` only — so removing the kind would silently reclassify historical records as `quarantine_unknown` without any version change to signal it. That is a worse outcome than a few lines of dead-looking code.
  - **`ADR-20260805-01`'s intake link is untouched.** `IntakeLinkTokenPath`, `KeyPrefix.IntakeLink`, and `POST /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/intake-link` are a different feature that happens to share the words "one-time link".
- Alternatives considered:
  - Delete everything in one change set: rejected. It would have removed `/start`'s integration and handler along with the Lambda hosting them, taking consultation start down until the next apply.
  - Keep the OTL routes as an unauthenticated-recipient path: rejected by the owner. Nothing calls them, Meet covers handing out a join address, and two writers of the same transition is a correctness liability.
  - Also delete `KeyPrefix.Otl` / `EntityType.Otl`: rejected, as above.
  - Also delete the classifier's `otl` kind: rejected, as above.
  - Leave `SessionItem` in a stub `lib/otl.ts`: rejected. A module retained only to hold a type that has nothing to do with its name is how the accidental coupling happened in the first place.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: one writer of `confirmed -> in_progress`, reached by one authenticated call, with no token to strand and no single-use page that locks a participant out.
  - Positive: one fewer Lambda, log group, integration, and three fewer routes.
  - Positive: `lib/sessions.ts` now owns the session vocabulary, so chat and CDS no longer import it through a retired feature.
  - Trade-offs: **an unauthenticated recipient can no longer be handed a consultation link at all.** Nothing needs this today, and Meet covers handing out a join address, but it is a capability removed rather than replaced.
  - Trade-offs: `handleIssueOtl` called `setIntakeQueueStatus(bookingId, 'in_progress')` and `/start` does not, so that side effect is gone. It was never reachable from the current frontend, which had stopped calling the OTL routes.
  - Trade-offs: the retired storage vocabulary and the classifier's `otl` kind remain, so "deleted entirely" is true of the feature but not literally of every mention. Both exceptions are load-bearing and named above.
  - Trade-offs: `architecture/DATA_MODEL.md` keeps the OTL access-pattern and item-shape sections, marked RETIRED, rather than deleting them. They document why the reserved prefix exists.
  - **Not qualified.** Backend typecheck, lint, 1003 tests across 80 files (down 9 and 1 file: the OTL recovery suite and one OTL handler probe, both removed with their subject); `npm run package:lambdas` produces 21 handlers, down from 22; contract lint and generated-type regeneration clean (`contracts:check` current); frontend typecheck clean, `next build` clean, lint 0 errors (52 pre-existing warnings), 671 tests across 97 files; `terraform fmt -check -recursive` clean; `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` 17 tests. **The Terraform deletion is not applied until `Deploy Dev` runs**, so the `bayanhealth-dev-otl` Lambda and its three routes still exist on the deployed API until then.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) after `Deploy Dev`, confirm the three OTL routes are gone from the dev API and that `/start` still answers; (b) residual `OTL#` items in `dev` self-drain within 15 minutes via TTL, so no cleanup script is needed; (c) `setIntakeQueueStatus` on consultation start was dropped with the OTL — decide whether `/start` should set it, which is a product question about the doctor's intake queue rather than a regression of this change.
- Related files: `backend/src/handlers/bookings.ts`, `backend/src/lib/sessions.ts`, `backend/src/lib/consultation-start.ts`, `backend/src/lib/dynamo.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/chat.ts`, `backend/src/lib/chat-access.ts`, `backend/src/lib/chat-messages.ts`, `backend/src/__tests__/security-wave5-exploration.test.ts`, `backend/package.json`, `backend/scripts/package-lambdas.mjs`, `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `infra/modules/http_api/main.tf`, `infra/modules/http_api/variables.tf`, `infra/modules/http_api/outputs.tf`, `infra/environments/{dev,staging,prod}/main.tf`, `infra/environments/{dev,staging,prod}/outputs.tf`, `architecture/DATA_MODEL.md`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/lib/api/consultSession.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/hooks/useStartConsultation.ts`

### ADR-20260809-01: Close the demo path — on-demand mode, a patient-readable prescription, and two silent dead ends

- Date: 2026-08-09
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally; Terraform route and Lambda env not applied until `Deploy Dev` runs. Not qualified in any environment.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: The target demo is one continuous story: patient books on-demand, doctor sees the request with the intake and accepts, both get chat and a meeting link, and after the consult the patient receives patient education and a prescription. Auditing the surface against that story found the middle and the end both broken, and the failures were silent rather than loud.

  **No frontend caller ever sent `bookingMode`.** `buildCreateBookingRequest` emitted `serviceType`, `scheduledAt`, `channel`, and three optionals. The handler defaults an absent mode to `scheduled`, so **every booking the product has ever created was scheduled** — which is why the on-demand request pool was permanently empty even with `ON_DEMAND_POOL_ENABLED = true` in dev, and why the on-demand waiting screen shipped in ADR-20260808-03 was unreachable the day it merged. The field was not even declared on `CreateBookingRequest` in the contract, so no client could have asked for the on-demand path.

  **A released prescription had no reader.** Generation, finalization, and release were all implemented; the only patient-readable CDS route was `patient-education/released`. `outputs/current` is doctor and admin-break-glass by design, and the legacy `prescription` consultation-document path is permanently retired (410). So "the doctor sends a prescription" ended at the doctor's own screen, with no route by which a patient could ever see it.

  **Two dead ends found on the way.** `STEPS` in the booking wizard has five entries and the render block only handled indices 0-3, so a `completed` booking showed the tracker with "Booked" lit above an **empty card** — silence at the exact moment the patient most needs to know what happens next. And `videoJoinUrl` was rendered only on the patient's booking page, so the assigned doctor could not see the meeting link from inside the consultation at all.
- Decision:
  - **Contract `bookingMode` on `CreateBookingRequest`, and send it explicitly on both paths.** `/booking/createBooking` (no doctor chosen) sends `on_demand`; the doctor/slot path sends `scheduled`. Neither relies on the backend default, because a default nobody chose is what produced the original defect. The builder still defaults to `scheduled` for a caller that says nothing, so an on-demand broadcast is never entered by omission.
  - **Generalize the patient-readable reader to a named allowlist** rather than copying the module. `released-patient-education.ts` becomes type-parameterized (`findReleasedArtifact`, `toReleasedArtifact`) over `PATIENT_READABLE_OUTPUT_TYPES = ['patient_education', 'prescription']`, with the patient-education-specific functions kept as thin wrappers so existing callers and tests are untouched.
  - **The allowlist is deliberately narrow.** `plan`, `final_icd`, `lab_request`, `imaging_request`, and `medical_certificate` are excluded — not because they are secret, but because each needs its own decision about presentation and accompanying explanation before being handed to a patient unmediated. Adding a type is an explicit act, not a consequence of a physician pressing release.
  - **One handler serves both patient reads.** `handleReleasedPatientArtifact(event, outputType)` keeps the authorization rule in one place: participant-only, read strongly-consistently from the canonical session projection, with unknown consultation / non-participant / nothing-released all collapsing to the same concealed `404`. Two copies of that rule would be two places for it to drift. The projector also pins `outputType`, so a pointer that somehow addressed another artifact cannot return one output type in another's place.
  - **The prescription route is documented as not dispensable**, in the contract and on the card: no prescriber identity, no licence number, no signature, not verifiable by a pharmacy.
  - **The patient client narrows the wire and never trusts it.** `fetchReleasedPrescription` validates the shape and returns `null` when it does not narrow. This is not defensive theatre — the first implementation read `.medications.length` off the response and the existing `PatientBookingDetail` tests caught it throwing during render, which would have taken down the patient's entire booking page: summary card, video link, chat entry, and education card with it. A prescription that cannot be displayed is a missing prescription; it is never a reason to lose the rest of the page.
  - **The UI refuses to present the deterministic fallback as medicine.** When the provider fails a gate, times out, or returns an empty completion, `protected-generation.ts` emits `genericName: "physician selection required"` with every other field `"not specified"` — and returns HTTP 200. The card detects that and renders "Your doctor has not finished this yet" with an instruction to message the doctor, instead of a drug and a dose. A mixed payload labels the offending line rather than hiding it, because silently dropping a row would misrepresent what the physician released. This is a presentation guard, not a substitute for physician review before release.
  - **Add the wizard's terminal step** (`CompletedStep`): consultation complete, doctor is writing up, you need not stay on this page, anything shared appears here. It does not promise a prescription, because releasing one is a discretionary per-artifact act that may never happen. `Booking.status` is carried onto the wizard model so the step can distinguish `completed` from `cancelled`, which `step` alone cannot.
  - **Render the video card inside `ConsultationRoom`**, which both participants reach, so the meeting link is symmetric. It reads `GET /v1/bookings/{bookingId}` because `/state` does not carry `videoJoinUrl`.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Infer on-demand from the absence of `preferredDoctorId`: rejected. That is the same class of implicit inference that produced the bug — and it was exactly the defect ADR-20260808-03 fixed on the read side (`detail.doctorId ? "regular" : "on-demand"`). The mode is a stated intention.
  - Make any released artifact patient-readable: rejected. Release and "appropriate to hand a patient unaccompanied" are different questions.
  - Add a patient branch to `handleCdsContractRoute`: rejected for the same reason ADR-20260806-04 rejected it — that boundary is the doctor-only safety gate for twenty-four operations and must not be widened so one read can work.
  - Duplicate `released-patient-education.ts` for prescriptions: rejected. Two copies of the participant-authorization rule is the kind of duplication that drifts silently in exactly the place it must not.
  - Revive the legacy `prescription` consultation document so the patient gets a real printable script: rejected here. It is deliberately retired (410) and reviving it would reintroduce the pre-assessment-first writer. A dispensable document needs prescriber identity, signature, and tamper-evidence, and is its own piece of work.
  - Suppress a fallback prescription entirely rather than labelling it: rejected. The physician released *something*; hiding it would make the patient's view disagree with the record.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the on-demand story is reachable end to end for the first time — pool, accept, waiting screen, cancel — all of which existed and none of which could be entered.
  - Positive: a prescription reaches the patient, closing the one gap that made "booking to prescription" undemonstrable.
  - Positive: the doctor and the patient now see the same meeting link from the same screen.
  - Positive: the patient's post-consult page says something instead of nothing.
  - Trade-offs: **the prescription is not a dispensable instrument.** A patient can read what was prescribed and cannot present it anywhere. Stated in the contract and on the card, but it is a real limit, not a cosmetic one.
  - Trade-offs: the placeholder guard is a string comparison against the deterministic template's `genericName`. If that template text changes, the guard silently stops matching. Both live in the repo and the test names the coupling, but it is a coupling.
  - Trade-offs: generated prescriptions remain ungrounded in the drug catalog — `protected-generation.ts` never consults it, and the only drug-data consumer in the CDS tree is the retired SOAP path. Validated for shape, not for pharmacology.
  - Trade-offs: `ConsultationRoom` now issues a second read per mount.
  - **Not qualified.** Backend typecheck, lint, 1003 tests across 80 files; contract lint and generated-type regeneration current; `terraform fmt -check -recursive`; `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` 17/17 including Terraform-route/OpenAPI parity for the new route; frontend typecheck, `next build`, lint 0 errors (52 pre-existing warnings), 683 tests across 99 files (12 new). The new route exists in the contract and the Terraform inventory and **is not applied** until `Deploy Dev` runs.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) render artifact payloads readably in the physician workspace instead of raw JSON; (b) ground prescription generation in the drug catalog and the `clinical_warning` entities; (c) decide whether a dispensable prescription document is in scope, which reopens prescriber identity, signature, and verification; (d) the ten-minute pool expiry from ADR-20260808-03 is still open, so an unclaimed request still waits indefinitely.
- Related files: `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `backend/src/lib/cds/released-patient-education.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/cds.ts`, `infra/modules/cds_operations/routes.tf`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/lib/createBookingRequest.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/patient/OnDemandBooking.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/doctor/DoctorBooking.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/consultation/CompletedStep.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/consultation/BookingWizard.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/lib/api/releasedPrescription.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/components/prescription/PrescriptionCard.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/components/session/ConsultationRoom.tsx`, `architecture/DEMO_SCRIPT.md`

### ADR-20260809-02: Move the CDS demo path to Qwen3.6-Plus

- Date: 2026-08-09
- Status: Accepted — applied to `dev` only via Terraform; **not qualified**. Supersedes ADR-20260624-01's model choice for `dev`; `staging` and `prod` remain on the `qwen2.5-7b` default.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: `Qwen/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-Turbo` has been the production-default baseline since ADR-20260624-01 and is being deprecated by the provider. It cannot remain the demo model for something being recorded and shown. `qwen3.6-plus` was already implemented as a selectable preset in `backend/src/lib/cds/profiles.ts` with a validated Terraform variable, so this is a configuration decision rather than an implementation one — which is exactly what the preset mechanism was built for.
- Decision:
  - **`cds_soap_model_preset = "qwen3.6-plus"` and `cds_patient_model_preset = "qwen3.6-plus"` in `dev`.** Both families, so the demo does not silently run two different models on the plan and the patient-education paths.
  - **`cds_soap_max_tokens` raised from 2048 to 4096 in `dev`.** The 2048 cap was a cost knob chosen for a non-reasoning model. A truncated completion is rejected as invalid and silently demoted to the deterministic placeholder template — which for a prescription means "physician selection required" reaching the screen with an HTTP 200. That is the single outcome a clinical demo must not hit, and 4096 is the preset's own default for the reason.
  - **`staging` and `prod` are untouched**, so this is a dev-scoped model change and not a platform default change.
  - The preset carries the two settings that make a reasoning model viable on the synchronous path, and both matter: `stream: true`, which the Together serverless endpoint requires outright and without which the request is rejected `400 streaming_required`; and `disableThinking`, without which the request spends its whole token budget on a reasoning channel the pipeline neither parses nor logs, returns empty `content`, and is demoted to the template.
- Alternatives considered:
  - `kimi-k2.6`: viable and faster (~4.3s vs ~17s measured), but Qwen3.6-Plus is the continuation of the Qwen line already in use, and the owner named it. Kimi remains selectable.
  - Keep `qwen2.5-7b` for the demo: rejected. It is being deprecated; demonstrating on a model that is going away is a false picture.
  - Set the slug directly via `CDS_SOAP_PRIMARY_MODEL`: rejected. It bypasses the preset's timeout, streaming, and thinking defaults, which are the parts that make the model work on the sync path.
  - Leave `cds_soap_max_tokens` at 2048: rejected, per the truncation reasoning above.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the demo runs on a current model, changed by two Terraform variables with no code change.
  - Positive: raising the token budget removes the most likely cause of a silent placeholder prescription mid-demo.
  - **Trade-offs: latency. ~17s measured against a 26s preset timeout, a 30s Lambda timeout, and a hard, non-configurable 30s API Gateway ceiling.** The margin is real but thin: a slow provider response now shows as a visible pause, and a bad one can still exhaust the budget. If a generation times out during the demo it degrades to the deterministic template with a 200, which is why the demo script tells the operator how to recognise that.
  - Trade-offs: 4096 tokens costs more per call than 2048. Accepted for `dev`.
  - Trade-offs: **this is not provider qualification.** Neither upgrade preset has been qualified, and a dev demo is not evidence for staging, canary, or production.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) measure the real latency distribution on the demo prompts before trusting the 26s budget for anything beyond a demo; (b) qualify a model properly before changing the `staging`/`prod` default; (c) consider whether the 30s ceiling makes the async continuation path mandatory for reasoning models rather than optional.
- Related files: `infra/environments/dev/main.tf`, `backend/src/lib/cds/profiles.ts`, `.kiro/steering/tech.md`, `architecture/AI_INFERENCE.md`, `architecture/DEMO_SCRIPT.md`

### ADR-20260809-03: Single-engineer team; UI/UX board handover

- Date: 2026-08-09
- Status: Accepted
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer, `ben.bayanhealth`)
- Context: Dev C, the sole frontend developer under ADR-20260630-01, is offboarded (Dev B already was). The remaining team is one engineer, `ben.bayanhealth`, plus a UI/UX designer who is handing over a fresh UI/UX board to be implemented later rather than contributing code. The governance documents still described a multi-developer split with cross-review rows, which no longer describes reality — and an ownership matrix that names reviewers who do not exist is worse than one that admits there are none.
- Decision:
  - **Dev A owns the entire technical surface**: backend, frontend, cloud, AI/CDS, DevOps, security, contracts, data/defaults, migration planning, merge operations, and production deployment operations.
  - **The UI/UX designer is not a delivery owner and not a review gate.** Their output is a board handed over for later implementation, tracked as a separate Dev A task rather than as design QA on in-flight work.
  - **State the consequence rather than paper over it: peer review is unavailable, not optional.** The controls that remain are automated — CI's six checks, contract and generated-type parity, `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py`, and the test suites — plus the evidence discipline in `DEFINITION_OF_DONE.md`. Every matrix row that previously depended on a second person now depends on those gates.
  - **Work that genuinely needs a second pair of eyes stays an external dependency.** Clinical content review, production enablement, and payment go-live are not satisfied by the sole engineer approving their own work, and the matrix must not imply otherwise.
  - ADR-20260728-01 still holds: named-person approval is not a repository implementation gate, and point-of-action authorization still governs every environment or data mutation.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Leave the matrix describing Dev C: rejected. A document naming absent reviewers invites a reader to believe a review happened.
  - Delete the review columns entirely: rejected. The distinction between "reviewed by automation" and "needs an external human" is the most useful thing left in the table.
  - Treat the designer as frontend review: rejected. Design QA is not code, security, or contract review, and labelling it as such would overstate the coverage.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the governance documents describe the actual team, so evidence claims can be read at face value.
  - Positive: automated gates are named as the review mechanism, which makes weakening them a visible act.
  - **Trade-offs: a single point of failure on the entire technical surface**, and no second reader for security-sensitive or clinical changes. This raises the value of the automated gates and the cost of skipping them; it is a risk to manage, not one this ADR removes.
  - Trade-offs: frontend velocity drops, and the incoming UI/UX board is queued work rather than parallel work.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) scope and schedule the UI/UX board implementation once handed over; (b) revisit branch protection, which is unavailable while the repo is private on the free plan and which matters more, not less, with one engineer.
- Related files: `architecture/REVIEW_AND_APPROVAL_MATRIX.md`, `.kiro/steering/product.md`, `.kiro/steering/structure.md`

### ADR-20260809-04: The on-demand pool flag never reached the Lambda that gates it

- Date: 2026-08-09
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally; not applied until `Deploy Dev` runs. Not qualified.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: Live testing found the on-demand flow still broken after ADR-20260809-01 wired `bookingMode` through: the patient's Finding step was skipped, the doctor's request pool read "0 open requests" while the dashboard showed six patients in the queue, and neither party could reach the chat room.

  **Root cause: a per-Lambda environment variable was set on the wrong Lambda.** `ON_DEMAND_POOL_ENABLED` was present only on `bayanhealth-dev-intake-queue`, which *serves* the pool routes. The decision about whether a paid booking *enters* the pool lives in `finalizeBookingAfterPaymentConfirm` (`lib/intake-queue.ts`), and that function is bundled into and executed by two **other** Lambdas: `payments` (the live gateway-hold path) and `payment-proof` (the legacy proof-review path). Neither had the variable.

  esbuild inlines shared libs per handler, so a flag gating a shared lib is per-Lambda configuration, not global. With it unset, `isOnDemandPoolEnabled()` returned false inside the payments Lambda, the bypass evaluated false, and every on-demand booking was auto-matched to a doctor at payment time. Everything downstream followed from that one silent false:

  - the pool filters `attribute_not_exists(doctorId)`, so an auto-assigned booking never appeared — the pool was **structurally** empty, not merely quiet;
  - the patient's wizard maps `confirmed` + `doctorId` to `confirmation`, so the **Finding step was skipped**, and the waiting screen and cancel control shipped in ADR-20260808-03 stayed unreachable;
  - no doctor ever *accepted* anything, so the Grab-style acceptance the product is built around never occurred.

  Nothing errored. No log line said the feature was off. Three ADRs' worth of on-demand work was inert because of one missing key in one environment block.

  **A second, independent defect surfaced once accept worked.** `handleAcceptRequest` hardcoded `intakeQueueStatus: 'pending'`, which lands a booking in the doctor board's "booking requests" column — whose only control is "Confirm & Send Intake". So a doctor who had just accepted a request with a **fully submitted intake** was asked to send the patient an intake form, and had no path to Start Consultation at all. The full Subjective/Objective card and the Start control both live in the "ready intakes" column.

  **A third: the doctor had neither the meeting link nor a route back to chat.** `videoJoinUrl` is disclosed to the owning patient *or the assigned doctor* from `confirmed` onward, and only the patient's booking page rendered it. The only doctor-side entry to the chat room was the redirect fired by "Start Consultation", so a doctor who navigated away could not return to the conversation.
- Decision:
  - **Set `ON_DEMAND_POOL_ENABLED` on `payments` and `payment_proof`** in addition to `intake_queue`. The rule is now stated in the Terraform comments: a flag gating a shared lib must be set on every Lambda that bundles it.
  - **Add a CI-gated regression guard.** `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` gains `OnDemandPoolFlagWiring`, which asserts every Lambda in a named `POOL_AWARE_LAMBDAS` list receives the flag *and* receives it from `var.on_demand_pool_enabled` rather than a hardcoded literal. The guard was verified to fail by removing the payments entry and re-running — a test that cannot fail is not a guard. This bug class is invisible at runtime, so it needed a static check rather than better logging.
  - **`claimOnDemandRequest` takes `intakeQueueStatus` from its caller**, and `handleAcceptRequest` derives it: `ready` when the patient's intake is already `submitted`, `pending` otherwise. An accepted request with a complete intake therefore lands in "ready intakes" with the full S/O card and a Start control, which is what the flow actually needs. An unsubmitted intake stays `pending`, which is correct — that doctor really does need to issue an intake link first.
  - **A failed intake read degrades to `pending`, never `ready`.** Claiming readiness on a failed read would offer Start Consultation for a consultation whose subjective/objective card may be empty. One extra step for the doctor is the cheaper error.
  - **`DoctorConsultationAccess` in the doctor's drawer**: the meeting link from `confirmed` onward, matching what the patient already has, plus a chat-room entry **only** once the booking is `in_progress`. Chat is gated server-side on `in_progress` plus an active session, so offering it earlier would present a control that returns `409`; the panel says what unlocks it instead.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Set the flag on every Lambda unconditionally: rejected. It would hide which handlers actually depend on it, and the named list is what makes the regression guard meaningful.
  - Read the flag from a shared config store instead of env: rejected as disproportionate. The env var is fine; the failure was not knowing which Lambdas needed it, and a static assertion fixes that directly.
  - Move `finalizeBookingAfterPaymentConfirm` out of `lib/intake-queue.ts` so the coupling is more obvious: worth doing eventually, but a refactor is a poor vehicle for a live bug fix.
  - Add a Start Consultation control to the "booking requests" drawer: rejected. It treats the symptom. The booking's queue status was simply wrong, and fixing the status fixes the column, the card, and the control together.
  - Unlock chat at `confirmed` so the doctor and patient can talk before starting: rejected here. Chat is consultation-scoped and the canonical CDS session is created by `/start`; loosening that gate is a clinical-record decision, not a UI convenience, and deserves its own ADR.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the on-demand flow works end to end for the first time — booking enters the pool, doctor sees it and accepts, patient sees the waiting screen with a working cancel.
  - Positive: accepting a request with a submitted intake now leads directly to review and start, with no misleading "send an intake form" detour.
  - Positive: the doctor and the patient see the same meeting link, and the doctor has a durable route back into the chat room.
  - Positive: the flag/Lambda mismatch is now a CI failure rather than a silent no-op.
  - Trade-offs: the accept path performs one extra DynamoDB read to check intake status. Bounded and once per acceptance.
  - Trade-offs: `POOL_AWARE_LAMBDAS` is a hand-maintained list. If a fourth handler starts bundling the bypass, the guard will not know. The comment names why each entry is there so the next reader can extend it.
  - Trade-offs: chat still requires the consultation to be started, so there is no pre-consult messaging. Deliberate, and named above as a separate decision.
  - **Not qualified.** Backend typecheck, lint, 1005 tests across 80 files (2 new covering the ready/pending decision and the failed-read fallback); frontend typecheck, 683 tests across 99 files; `terraform fmt -check -recursive`; `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` 19 tests (2 new). The Terraform env change is **not applied** until `Deploy Dev` runs, so on-demand bookings in dev continue to be auto-matched until then.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) re-run the full demo path in dev after the deploy and confirm a booking actually lands in the pool; (b) consider moving `finalizeBookingAfterPaymentConfirm` out of `lib/intake-queue.ts`, since a payment-path function living in an intake-queue module is what made the Lambda coupling hard to see; (c) decide separately whether pre-consult chat should exist.
- Related files: `infra/modules/http_api/main.tf`, `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py`, `backend/src/lib/on-demand-pool.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/intake-queue.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/on-demand-pool.test.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/components/homepage/DoctorConsultationAccess.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/components/homepage/DoctorDashboardDrawer.tsx`

### ADR-20260809-05: Pre-consult chat — open the conversation at assignment, not at start

- Date: 2026-08-09
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally; not applied until `Deploy Dev` runs. Not qualified. Amends the chat gate established under ADR-20260806-01.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: Chat was gated on the booking being `in_progress` **and** having an active consultation session — the state produced by `POST /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/start`. That meant the two people who had just been matched to each other, one of whom had already paid, had no channel at all until the doctor pressed Start. A patient running late, remembering something they left off the intake form, or unsure how to join the call had no way to say so; a doctor wanting to ask one clarifying question before opening the room had the same problem.

  I flagged this as needing its own decision rather than folding it into ADR-20260809-04, because the gate is a clinical-record boundary and not a UI convenience: messages are keyed to a consultation session, and the canonical CDS session is created by `/start` and ended by `/complete` to initialize the Assessment. Attaching pre-consult messages to a consultation that has not happened would misstate the record.

  Two facts made a clean answer possible. First, **message reads are already booking-scoped**: `listChatMessagesByBooking` queries gsi1 on `BOOKING#<bookingId>` with a `MESSAGE#` prefix, and only the base-table partition key uses `sessionId`. Second, `useConsultationChat` is already keyed on `bookingId` and already has an HTTP fallback — the frontend never needed a session id.
- Decision:
  - **Chat opens when the booking is `confirmed` with an assigned doctor, and stays open through `in_progress`.** Two conditions, not one: `confirmed` alone is insufficient, because a pooled on-demand request nobody has claimed has no counterparty and no sender the server could authorize as the doctor side.
  - **A pre-consult message belongs to the booking and to no session.** It is partitioned under `BOOKING#<bookingId>` via a new `chatMessageBookingPrimaryKey`, and carries **no** `sessionId` and **no** `consultationId`. Inventing either would attach clinical conversation to a consultation record that does not exist. Both fields become optional on `BookingMessage`, and `toPublicChatMessage` omits them rather than sending null.
  - **In-consult writes are unchanged.** With a session present the message is still partitioned under `SESSION#<sessionId>` with both ids. Both layouts write identical gsi1 keys, so the two phases read back as **one ordered conversation** and messages already written under `SESSION#` partitions stay readable. No migration.
  - **Two gate functions, not one flag.** `requireChatBookingContext` is permissive and returns `session: SessionItem | null`; `requireLiveChatBookingContext` returns a non-null session by type. Messages (list, send) and `GET /state` use the permissive one; **presence, typing, and the WebSocket use the strict one**, because all three address a session id and have nothing to address without it. Separate functions rather than a boolean so a caller cannot forget the check and so the type carries the guarantee.
  - **Realtime remains in-consult only; pre-consult is polled.** `POST /messages` persists a pre-consult message and skips the broadcast, because there is no channel to emit on. Stated in the UI rather than hidden: a participant who does not know they are on a short delay reads a slow reply as being ignored.
  - **The HTTP transport now polls every 3s.** This was a pre-existing defect independent of this change: the fallback hydrated exactly once, so anyone who fell back to HTTP saw their own messages and never the other side's until they reloaded. Polling runs only while `transport === "http"` — on the WebSocket the server pushes, and polling too would double every message's arrival path.
  - **`GET /state` answers in the pre-consult phase** with `session` absent, so a successful response no longer means "the consultation is live" — `booking.status` means that. The room polls until a session appears, so it upgrades itself from polled to realtime the moment the doctor starts.
  - **Chat closes again at `completed`.** Not widened here. Post-consult read access is a records-retention question, not a chat question.
  - **The assigned doctor gets a Start control inside the room**, so a doctor who opened it to answer one question does not have to navigate back to the dashboard to begin.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Create the consultation session at acceptance so chat has one: **rejected**, and this is the important rejection. It would make the canonical CDS session — the thing `/complete` ends to initialize the Assessment — begin before the consultation did, corrupting the clinical timeline that ADR-20260703-01 depends on. Convenience is not worth moving a clinical boundary.
  - Re-key the whole chat channel (messages, WS connections, presence) from `sessionId` to `bookingId`: this is the *correct* long-term model — the room is already booking-keyed and the sessionId keying is an OTL-era leftover — but it touches the realtime path, presence, and the WS registry at once, for a feature that currently works in-consult. Deferred as a follow-up rather than done inside a feature change.
  - Allow the WebSocket to subscribe pre-consult using `bookingId` as the channel: rejected for now. The channel id would then *change* when the consultation starts, stranding any client connected before it.
  - Leave the HTTP fallback non-polling and accept send-only pre-consult chat: rejected. A chat where you cannot see replies is not chat.
  - Open chat at `pending_payment`: rejected. No doctor is assigned and nothing has been paid; there is no counterparty.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the patient and their assigned doctor can talk from the moment they are matched, which is the point the product introduces them to each other.
  - Positive: one continuous conversation across pre-consult, in-consult, and the doctor's post-consult review, with no migration and no split history.
  - Positive: a real latent bug is fixed — the HTTP fallback now refreshes, which matters in-consult too whenever the WebSocket fails.
  - Positive: the room no longer dead-ends on "hasn't started yet", and upgrades itself to realtime when the consultation begins.
  - **Trade-offs: pre-consult chat has no presence, no typing indicator, and up to ~3s message latency.** Structural, because the realtime channel is session-keyed. Named in the UI.
  - Trade-offs: a booking-partition write and a session-partition write now coexist for the same logical entity. Reads are unaffected because they go through gsi1, but a reader of the base table sees `MESSAGE#` items in two places. The channel re-key above is what would eventually collapse that.
  - Trade-offs: `sessionId` and `consultationId` are now optional on `BookingMessage`, so every client must treat them as absent-capable. The contract says so, and `bookingId` is the field that is always present.
  - **Trade-off worth naming plainly: the clinical record now contains messages that precede the consultation.** They are attributable, timestamped, and participant-scoped, but they sit outside the consultation session and outside the CDS clinical-input snapshot — so they are *not* part of the safety evidence the assessment-first pipeline reasons over. A physician who receives material clinical information in pre-consult chat must still record it through intake or the Assessment; the chat is not a clinical input channel. Making it one would be a much larger decision.
  - **Not qualified.** Backend typecheck, lint, 1021 tests across 81 files (16 new, a dedicated `chat-access.test.ts` covering the gate's edges: pre-consult open, unassigned refused, pre-payment and post-completion refused, non-participant 404 rather than 403, and the strict variant refusing both pre-consult and a missing session); frontend typecheck, `next build`, lint 0 errors, 683 tests across 99 files; contract types regenerated.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) re-key the chat channel from `sessionId` to `bookingId` so messages, presence, and WS connections share one identity and pre-consult can be realtime; (b) decide whether the conversation should remain readable after `completed`; (c) decide whether pre-consult messages should feed the CDS clinical input, which is a clinical-safety decision and not a transport one.
- Related files: `backend/src/lib/chat-access.ts`, `backend/src/lib/chat-access.test.ts`, `backend/src/lib/chat-messages.ts`, `backend/src/lib/dynamo.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/chat.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/chat-ws.ts`, `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/hooks/useConsultationChat.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/components/session/ConsultationRoom.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/lib/api/consultSession.ts`

### ADR-20260809-06: An explicit NULL `doctorId` made every booking look assigned to DynamoDB

- Date: 2026-08-09
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally; not applied until `Deploy Dev` runs. Not qualified.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: After ADR-20260809-04 the patient side worked — the Finding step appeared and the waiting screen polled correctly — but the doctor's request pool was **still** empty. Live inspection of the dev table settled it: the patient's on-demand booking existed, was `on_demand`, and had `doctorId` stored as a DynamoDB **NULL**, not absent.

  `handleCreateBooking` wrote `doctorId: null` literally. DynamoDB stores that as a NULL-*typed attribute that exists*. Every "is this booking unassigned?" test in the system is `attribute_not_exists(doctorId)` — and against a NULL attribute that is **false**. So a freshly created booking read as unassigned to every TypeScript caller (`toApiBooking` checks `typeof item.doctorId === 'string'`, which correctly rejects null) and as **assigned** to the database.

  Two consequences, both silent:
  - `listOnDemandRequestPool` filtered out every candidate. The pool was empty **by construction**, not merely quiet — no amount of new bookings would ever have populated it.
  - `claimOnDemandRequest` could never satisfy its condition, so even a visible request could not have been accepted.

  This is the second silent-nothing bug in the same feature, and the shape is worth naming: the first was a flag absent from the Lambda that read it, this one is a value whose *type* defeated the predicate that read it. Neither errored, neither logged, and both presented as "the feature just does not happen".

  **Two unrelated board-label defects surfaced from the same report.** The doctor saw five identical cards reading `Ref 00D81D / General Consultation / Just now / Scheduled` and said no bookings had been made in that window. Both were real:
  - `toPatientBoardInfo` derived the card's reference from **`booking.patientId`**, not the booking id. The dev patient's id ends `…00d81d`, so all five of their bookings rendered the identical reference — five distinct bookings looked like one card repeated five times. `RequestPool` already derived it from the booking id; the board disagreed.
  - `getRelativeTimeStringFromTimestamp` only subtracted: `Date.now() - timestamp`. The board feeds it `scheduledAt`, and all five bookings were scheduled **in the future** (28-30 Aug against 9 Aug), so the value went negative, fell through the `seconds < 5` branch, and every upcoming appointment rendered as **"Just now"**. A board whose normal case is a future instant had no future branch at all.
- Decision:
  - **Stop writing `doctorId: null`.** `BookingItem.doctorId` becomes optional and the create path omits it. Absent is the only representation of "unassigned".
  - **Recognise both shapes wherever "unassigned" is tested**, in the pool filter and the claim condition: `attribute_not_exists(doctorId) OR attribute_type(doctorId, :nullType)`. Not defensive padding — every booking already in the table has the NULL form, so without this clause the fix would only help bookings created after the deploy and the existing ones would stay invisible forever.
  - **Do not weaken the claim's compare-and-set.** Widening "unassigned" must not become "assign unconditionally": the pool is broadcast, several doctors can press Accept in the same second, and the condition still pins `attribute_exists(pk)`, the status, and the mode. A test asserts those survive.
  - **No data migration.** The `OR` clause makes existing rows work as they are, and normal assignment overwrites the NULL with a string. Migrating would touch live bookings for no behavioural gain.
  - **The board's reference comes from the booking id**, matching `RequestPool`. A field labelled `Ref` on a booking card has to identify the booking.
  - **Relative time becomes symmetric**, with a real future branch (`in 45 mins`, `Tomorrow`, `in 19 days`) and `null` for an unparseable input so a caller can omit the line rather than print `NaN days ago`.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Only stop writing null, without the `OR` clause: rejected. It leaves every existing booking permanently invisible to the pool, which is most of the dev data and would have looked like the bug was not fixed.
  - Migrate the existing rows to remove the NULL attribute: rejected as unnecessary risk. A one-clause predicate change achieves the same result without writing to live bookings.
  - Test the condition by asserting against source text: written, then **thrown away**. It asserted that a string appears in a file, which passes whether or not the code works. Replaced with tests that inspect the actual `QueryCommand`/`UpdateCommand` input — DynamoDB evaluates the condition, so the command is the part this process owns and the part that was wrong.
  - Clamp negative elapsed time to "Just now": rejected. That is what it already effectively did. A future appointment is not "now"; it needed a direction, not a floor.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: an on-demand booking now actually appears in the pool, and can be claimed — the feature is reachable end to end for the first time.
  - Positive: existing dev bookings become visible without a migration, so the demo does not need fresh data to show a populated pool.
  - Positive: the board stops presenting distinct bookings as duplicates, and stops labelling appointments weeks away as having just arrived.
  - Trade-offs: two representations of "unassigned" are now tolerated in queries, which is one more thing a future reader must know. The comments say why, and new writes only ever produce one of them, so the legacy clause has a finite life.
  - Trade-offs: `doctorId` moving from `string | null` to optional is a type change on a widely-read item. Typecheck is clean, and every reader already narrowed with `typeof === 'string'` rather than trusting null.
  - **Not qualified.** Backend typecheck, lint, 1026 tests across 82 files (5 new asserting both unassigned shapes are accepted and that the atomic guard survives); frontend typecheck, `next build`, lint 0 errors, 689 tests across 100 files (6 new pinning the future/past/boundary cases of relative time); `terraform fmt`; infra tests 19.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) re-verify against live dev after the deploy that a paid on-demand booking appears in the pool and can be accepted — the previous two fixes each looked complete and were not; (b) audit for other `attribute_not_exists` predicates over fields that are written as explicit nulls, since this class of bug is invisible; (c) the board still shows `scheduledAt` as its card time, which for a pooled on-demand request is a near-meaningless "shortly from now" placeholder — worth showing request time instead.
- Related files: `backend/src/handlers/bookings.ts`, `backend/src/lib/on-demand-pool.ts`, `backend/src/lib/on-demand-pool.unassigned.test.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/hooks/usePatientBoard.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/lib/utils.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/lib/relative-time.test.ts`

### ADR-20260809-07: An unused expression value made every accept fail, and the patient had no door to the chat

- Date: 2026-08-09
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally; not applied until `Deploy Dev` runs. Not qualified.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: The pool populated and Accept appeared to work, but the booking then **disappeared from the doctor's side entirely** — gone from the pool, absent from their queue, visible to nobody. Separately, the patient had no way to reach the chat room.

  **The accept failure, from the CloudWatch log rather than inference:**

  ```
  accept on-demand request failed: ValidationException: Value provided in
  ExpressionAttributeValues unused in expressions: keys: {:null}
      at routeAssignmentChangeForBooking
  ```

  `routeAssignmentChangeForBooking` builds its condition two ways: when the booking has no doctor it uses `(attribute_not_exists(doctorId) OR doctorId = :null)`, and when it does it uses `doctorId = :expectedDoctorId`. But it passed **`':null': null` unconditionally**, and DynamoDB rejects the entire request with a 400 when a supplied expression value is never referenced.

  So the function was broken for every call where a doctor was already attached. The auto-match path always called it with none, so `:null` was used and it worked — which is why this stayed hidden until the request pool went live. On the accept path `claimOnDemandRequest` writes `doctorId` first, so the follow-up mirror call hit the unused-value error every single time.

  **Why that made the booking vanish rather than error visibly.** The claim had already committed, so the booking had a `doctorId` and had therefore left the pool (which filters unassigned). The gsi2 index keys — written only by the failing follow-up — were never set, so it never entered the doctor's queue either, which reads the `DOCTOR#<id>` partition. A live table read confirmed exactly that state: `status: confirmed`, `doctorId` set, `acceptedAt` set, `intakeQueueStatus: ready`, and `gsi2pk`/`gsi2sk` **absent**. Two surfaces, both blind, no error to the user.

  **The patient's missing door** was my own omission from ADR-20260809-05. I opened chat at doctor assignment on the server and gave the doctor an entry, but `PatientBookingDetail` still rendered its join card only when `status === 'in_progress'`. The server allowed the conversation and no patient screen offered a way in.
- Decision:
  - **Pass `:null` only when the condition references it.** `...(currentDoctorId ? { ':expectedDoctorId': currentDoctorId } : { ':null': null })`. This also repairs reassignment, which had the same latent break.
  - **`claimOnDemandRequest` writes `gsi2pk`/`gsi2sk` in the same conditional update that assigns the doctor.** The index and the assignment can no longer diverge: a claim either fully lands or does not happen. Splitting them across two calls is what allowed an assigned-but-unqueryable booking to exist at all, and no amount of fixing the second call removes that window.
  - **`scheduledAt` for the index sort key is read from the stored booking**, never taken from the client.
  - **The CDS assignment mirror becomes best-effort, with a reconciliation log.** After the claim the booking is already fully assigned and visible, so a mirror failure must not present as a failed accept. Before, it propagated a 500 while the booking had already left the pool — the worst combination: the user is told it failed and the state says it half-succeeded.
  - **The patient's card renders from `confirmed` with an assigned doctor**, in two states: "Message your doctor / Open chat" before the consultation starts, and "Your consultation has started / Join consultation" after. The pre-consult wording deliberately does not claim the consultation has begun.
  - **No card while the booking is unassigned**, matching the server: a pooled request nobody has claimed has no one to message.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Drop the `:null` value entirely and always compare `doctorId = :expectedDoctorId`: rejected. The unassigned branch genuinely needs to match both an absent and a NULL `doctorId` — the same legacy shape ADR-20260809-06 dealt with.
  - Leave the index write in the follow-up call and just fix the ValidationException: rejected. It fixes this instance and preserves the window; any future failure in that call recreates an invisible booking.
  - Keep the mirror failure fatal so the problem is loud: rejected. Loud is good, but not by reporting failure for an operation that committed. The reconciliation log is the loud part; the response tells the truth.
  - Have the patient card appear for any `confirmed` booking regardless of assignee: rejected. It would offer a chat the server refuses with a `409`.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: accepting a request now completes, and the booking appears in the accepting doctor's queue.
  - Positive: an accepted booking can no longer be assigned-but-unqueryable, because both facts are written together.
  - Positive: reassignment is fixed as a side effect — it had the same unused-value break and nothing had exercised it.
  - Positive: the patient can finally reach the conversation, which is what made pre-consult chat real rather than only server-side.
  - Trade-offs: the CDS assignment mirror can now fail silently from the user's perspective. It logs `accept_assignment_mirror_failed` with the ids for reconciliation, and the clinical consequence is bounded — the canonical session does not exist pre-consult, so the mirror is a no-op in the ordinary accept case.
  - Trade-offs: bookings already stranded by this bug stay stranded. They have a `doctorId` and no gsi2 keys, so they are in neither surface, and nothing in the running system will pick them up. `bk_mslr9zge4qgmkoogads` in dev is one. They need either an operator index backfill or to be abandoned in favour of a fresh booking; no automatic repair is included, because writing to live bookings deserves its own authorization rather than riding along with a bug fix.
  - **Not qualified.** Backend typecheck, lint, 1027 tests across 82 files (1 new asserting the index keys are written in the same command as the assignment, plus updated accept assertions); frontend typecheck, `next build`, lint 0 errors, 690 tests across 100 files (the patient join-card test rewritten to the new rule, plus a new one pinning that an unassigned booking gets no entry).
  - Follow-up actions: (a) re-verify live that accept lands the booking in the doctor's queue — three consecutive fixes have each looked complete and not been; (b) decide whether to backfill the stranded dev bookings' index keys; (c) audit other `ExpressionAttributeValues` built conditionally against a fixed expression, since this failure mode is a 400 that only appears on the branch nothing exercised.
- Related files: `backend/src/lib/cds/session-lifecycle.ts`, `backend/src/lib/on-demand-pool.ts`, `backend/src/lib/on-demand-pool.unassigned.test.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/intake-queue.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/on-demand-pool.test.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/booking/components/patient/PatientBookingDetail.tsx`

### ADR-20260809-08: Accepting a pooled request had no way to tell the board it happened

- Date: 2026-08-09
- Status: Accepted — frontend-only; not applied until `Deploy Dev` runs (Amplify build). Not qualified.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: With ADR-20260809-07 deployed, the backend now assigns the doctor and writes the index keys atomically. Live testing still reported the booking vanishing on Accept — but this time it was, correctly, a **frontend** bug: the API call succeeds, the request leaves the pool, and it simply never appears anywhere else.

  `usePatientBoard` — the hook behind the doctor's "booking requests / pending / ready" columns — was never a React Query consumer. It kept its board in local `useState`, populated by one `api.get` fired from a `useEffect` on mount, with a hand-rolled optimistic-update/revert for its own accept action (`confirm` on the intake queue). It had no subscription to any cache, because it was not backed by a cache at all.

  `RequestPool`'s accept flow, correctly, calls `queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ["doctor-intake-queue"] })` after a successful pool accept — the pool and the board are separate components on the same dashboard, and this is the intended way for one to tell the other something changed. But invalidating a query key does nothing if no query is registered under that key. `usePatientBoard` had no `useQuery` at all, so this call was invalidating a cache entry that did not exist — a complete no-op, with no error, because asking React Query to invalidate an absent key is not a failure condition.

  So the actual sequence on accept was: `POST /accept` succeeds → the booking leaves the pool (it fails the pool's `attribute_not_exists(doctorId)` filter now that one is set) → `RequestPool`'s own list re-renders without it → the invalidation call fires and matches nothing → the board never re-fetches → the booking is assigned, correct, and visible to nobody in the UI. This is the fourth distinct failure mode in this feature, and each has been in a different layer: a Lambda env var, a DynamoDB NULL, an unused expression value, and now a missing subscription between two sibling components.
- Decision:
  - **Rewrite `usePatientBoard` onto React Query**, with its board read as `useQuery({ queryKey: [DOCTOR_INTAKE_QUEUE_QUERY_KEY, idToken], ... })` and its accept action as a `useMutation` using the library's own optimistic-update primitives (`onMutate` snapshot, `onError` rollback, `onSettled` refetch) rather than a hand-rolled `useState` copy of the same idea.
  - **Export `DOCTOR_INTAKE_QUEUE_QUERY_KEY` as a named constant** and have `RequestPool` invalidate that constant instead of the string literal `"doctor-intake-queue"` it already had. The literal was correct by coincidence — it happened to match what the key *would* be — but nothing enforced that the two ever agreed, and a rename on either side would have reintroduced this exact bug silently. A shared constant makes the two call sites fail to compile rather than fail at runtime if they ever diverge.
  - **Keep a 5-second poll on the board query** in addition to the invalidation. `RequestPool`'s invalidation covers the specific case of this doctor accepting from the pool; it covers nothing for a booking that reaches the queue by auto-match, or for a second doctor's dashboard. The board should not depend on a sibling component firing an invalidation to ever become correct.
  - **Drop `completePatient`.** It was dead code — no non-test caller existed anywhere in the frontend — and carrying it through the rewrite would have meant maintaining a`useState`-shaped API on top of a React Query-shaped implementation for a function nothing calls.
  - **Test the cross-component contract directly**, not just each hook's internals: `usePatientBoard.test.tsx` gains a case that fetches empty, then simulates a pool acceptance by invalidating the shared key and asserting the newly-available booking appears — this is the scenario that was silently broken. `RequestPool.test.tsx` is new and asserts the invalidation call uses the exported constant, so a future rename of the board's key is a compile error in the pool, not a silent behavioural regression discovered again by a doctor.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Have `RequestPool` call `usePatientBoard`'s `reload` directly via a prop or context: rejected. It would couple two independently-mounted dashboard sections through an imperative handle for something a shared cache key already solves more simply, and it would not help the second-doctor or auto-match cases at all.
  - Leave `usePatientBoard` on local state and just poll it unconditionally: would have fixed the symptom (eventually) but left the invalidation call in `RequestPool` doing nothing, which is exactly the kind of dead-looking-correct code that produced this bug in the first place — a future reader would have no reason to suspect it was inert.
  - Merge `RequestPool` and `usePatientBoard` into one component/hook: rejected as disproportionate. They render different sections of the dashboard for different reasons (unclaimed pool vs. this doctor's own queue) and forcing them into one hook would entangle two access patterns that are legitimately different.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: accepting from the pool now makes the booking appear on the board without waiting for the poll, because the invalidation is no longer a no-op.
  - Positive: the board is also correct for cases the pool's invalidation never covered — auto-matched bookings, and a second doctor's own dashboard — because of the added poll.
  - Positive: the query-key coupling between two sibling components is now enforced by the type system instead of by two developers remembering to keep a string literal in sync.
  - Trade-offs: the board now polls every 5s while mounted, matching the pool's existing cadence, which is a small steady-state cost for a page that was previously silent between manual reloads.
  - Trade-offs: `usePatientBoard`'s public shape (`board`, `isConnecting`, `error`, `isEmpty`, `acceptPatient`, `reload`) is preserved for its one consumer, `DoctorDashboardClient`, but the mutation is now `mutateAsync`-backed, so a caller awaiting `acceptPatient` observes React Query's rejection behaviour rather than a hand-written try/catch — functionally equivalent here, but worth knowing if another caller is added.
  - **Not qualified.** Frontend typecheck, `next build`, lint 0 new errors (52 pre-existing warnings, unchanged), 692 tests across 101 files (7 new: the cross-component regression case in `usePatientBoard.test.tsx`, and a new `RequestPool.test.tsx` asserting the shared-constant invalidation). No backend or infrastructure change in this fix.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) re-verify live in dev that a pool-accepted booking now appears in the accepting doctor's queue without a manual refresh; (b) audit other pairs of sibling components that communicate only through `queryClient.invalidateQueries` with a string literal on each side, since this is the second time a key mismatch of this shape has caused a silent no-op (the first was the `ON_DEMAND_POOL_ENABLED` Lambda-env mismatch in ADR-20260809-04, a different mechanism but the same "two places must agree and nothing checks that they do" shape).
- Related files: `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/hooks/usePatientBoard.ts`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/hooks/usePatientBoard.test.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/components/RequestPool.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/doctor/components/RequestPool.test.tsx`

### ADR-20260809-09: "Start consultation" checked whether the state read succeeded, not whether a session existed

- Date: 2026-08-09
- Status: Accepted — frontend-only; not applied until the next Amplify build. Not qualified.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: With ADR-20260809-05 through -08 deployed, both participants could reach the chat room and the Google Meet link before the consultation started. Live testing then reported the next step dead: pressing **Start consultation**, from either the room or the doctor's dashboard drawer, did nothing — no error, no navigation change worth noticing, no session created.

  `useStartConsultation.start()` begins by asking "is a session already running?" so a second click, or a resumed consultation, joins rather than re-starting:

  ```ts
  const existing = await fetchBookingConsultationState(idToken, bookingId).catch(() => null);
  if (existing) return enterRoom();
  ```

  That check was written when `GET /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/state` answered successfully **only** once a session existed — `existing` truthy and "a session is running" were the same fact. ADR-20260809-05 changed that: the route now also answers successfully for the **pre-consult** phase, returning `{ booking, session: undefined }` for a `confirmed` booking with an assigned doctor, specifically so the two participants could load their conversation before the consultation starts.

  `existing` is therefore truthy the moment a doctor is assigned, long before anyone has started anything. The hook read that as "already running," called `enterRoom()`, and navigated back to the exact screen the doctor was already on — **without ever calling `POST /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/start`**. No request reached the server at all.
