  7. **One `rate(1 minute)` EventBridge rule is ungated.** `infra/modules/cds_operations/transport.tf` schedules the 16-shard audit-outbox reconciliation every minute in every environment, including a dev that is idle between sessions — roughly 43,200 invocations a month plus shard queries and log volume. `infra/modules/notifications` gates precisely this pattern, and its variable description even quantifies it, so the omission is an inconsistency rather than a considered choice.
- Decision:
  - **Delete the `backend/dist` cache from all three workflows and package unconditionally.** This is the one item that reverses an ADR-20260817-01 decision: that pass added a `cache-hit` guard to `ci.yml` to match the deploy workflows, on the reasonable assumption that the cache was a saving. Measurement showed the guard was protecting a net loss and simultaneously enabling the placeholder-deploy path, so both the cache and the guard are removed rather than tuned. Packaging in each workflow also guarantees the zips being uploaded were built from the checked-out commit.
  - **Pin the bundle mtime before archiving** in `backend/scripts/package-lambdas.mjs` (`FIXED_MTIME`, `2020-01-01T00:00:00Z`, chosen because zip's DOS timestamp cannot represent anything before 1980). The archive becomes a pure function of the bundle bytes, so `source_code_hash` changes if and only if the code changes. Verified in three directions: two clean builds produced 21 of 21 identical hashes; an emitted source change produced a new hash; reverting that change restored the original hash exactly. A side effect worth noting is that comment-only edits now produce no Lambda update at all, since esbuild strips comments from the bundle.
  - **`timeout-minutes` on all seven jobs**: 15 backend, 20 frontend, 10 contract, 20 terraform, 45 both deploys, 60 destroy. Each is set well above observed duration — these are runaway guards, not targets, and the deploy limits stay generous because those jobs contain their own bounded 15-minute Amplify poll and smoke-retry ladder.
  - **`paths-ignore` on both `ci.yml` triggers**, covering `.kiro/**`, `architecture/**`, and binary documents — with `!architecture/CDS_SECURITY_OPERATIONS.md` re-included because `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` reads that runbook and asserts on its contents. Every other CI input was audited before the list was written: no CI-invoked script or test reads anything else under `architecture/` or `.kiro/`, while `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `infra/**`, `.github/workflows/**`, and both source trees are read by tests and are never ignored. Two documented semantics are relied on — a run is skipped only when *every* changed path matches, so mixed docs+code commits still run; and a later `!` pattern removes an earlier match.
  - **Duplicate the `paths-ignore` list rather than share it via a YAML anchor.** Actions gained anchor and alias support in 2025 but still rejects merge keys, and a workflow trigger cannot be tested before it lands on the default branch. Five repeated lines are preferable to depending on parser behaviour for something whose failure mode is a silently skipped run.
  - **Cache `.next/cache` in the CI frontend job**, keyed on the frontend lockfile plus source with `restore-keys` for partial reuse. Kept despite the transfer-cost argument that removed the Lambda cache, because this one is consumed by the very next step in the same job and nothing deploys from it: a stale restore degrades speed, not correctness.
  - **Make the audit-reconciliation interval a variable and set dev to `rate(5 minutes)`.** The sweep is deliberately *not* given a `count` that removes it, which is where this departs from the notifications module it superficially resembles. The notifications rule drives a product feature, so disabling it in dev loses nothing; this rule drains the CDS audit outbox, so disabling it in the environment where CDS is actively developed would let the backlog grow unattended, stop exercising the outbox depth and age metrics, and make a genuine outbox defect look like an idle queue — the exact misreading ADR-20260804-02 records, where 37 real DLQ messages were dismissed as known noise. Frequency is therefore the cost lever and the sweep itself is not optional. A `validation` block caps the variable at `rate(1 minute)` or `rate(5 minutes)` so no environment can widen it further; 5 minutes is the ceiling because the `AuditOutboxOldestAgeSeconds` warning alarm threshold is 300s and a slower sweep would trip it on schedule alone.
- Alternatives considered:
  - **Keep the `dist` cache but cache only the zips (8.0 MB, not 46 MB).** Rejected. It narrows the transfer but preserves the placeholder-deploy path, and it still moves 8 MB to save 1.7s. Once the measurement is in hand there is nothing left for the cache to buy.
  - **Add per-handler content hashing so only changed Lambdas are rebuilt.** Rejected as unnecessary: the whole 21-handler pass is 1.7s, so incremental packaging optimizes something already below noise. Deterministic zips deliver the actual benefit — incremental *deploys* — without the bookkeeping.
  - **Set `minify: true` or mark `@aws-sdk/*` as `external` to shrink bundles.** Rejected, and deliberately out of scope. The largest zip is 617 KB against a 50 MB direct-upload ceiling, so size is not a constraint; and relying on the runtime-provided SDK would silently couple handler behaviour to whatever version the Lambda runtime ships. That is a runtime-behaviour decision, not a build-pipeline tweak.
  - **Disable the audit-reconciliation rule in dev, matching `reminder_schedule_enabled`.** Rejected for the reason given above; this was the original recommendation from the sweep and it was changed once the rule's role as an audit-integrity mechanism, rather than a feature schedule, was accounted for.
  - **Collapse the three-leg Terraform matrix into one job.** Deferred, not rejected. ADR-20260817-01 turned this down because it does not remove the provider downloads and it loses the per-environment attribution that `fail-fast: false` exists to provide. That reasoning holds for wall-clock, but three runners each installing Node and Terraform to run one `terraform validate` is three times the billable minutes, and Actions minutes are metered on this repository's plan. It is a genuine cost/attribution trade-off and belongs with the provider-lock work, not bundled into this pass.
  - **A blanket `**/*.md` ignore.** Rejected: it would have silently included `architecture/CDS_SECURITY_OPERATIONS.md`, letting a runbook edit break `test_cds_infrastructure.py` with no run to catch it.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: three workflows stop moving 46 MB per run, and the only path that could deploy `lambda_placeholder.zip` as real handler code is gone.
  - Positive: `terraform apply` now updates only the Lambda functions whose code actually changed, and `terraform plan` output stops carrying 21 unconditional in-place updates — which matters most because that output is truncated to `tail -100`.
  - Positive: no job can exceed its limit; a wedged deploy releases its concurrency group in 45 minutes instead of 6 hours.
  - Positive: docs-only and steering-only commits skip four jobs. On `main` they also skip the deploy, since `deploy-dev.yml` triggers on CI completing — correct for a commit with nothing to deploy.
  - Positive: once applied, dev's audit reconciliation drops from ~43,200 to ~8,640 invocations a month, an 80% reduction, with the integrity mechanism still live.
  - Trade-off: packaging now runs in every workflow instead of being restored, adding ~1.7s plus process startup to each deploy. This is the intended exchange for removing the 46 MB transfer and the placeholder hole.
  - Trade-off: the `paths-ignore` list is a maintenance obligation. Any future check that reads a file under `architecture/` or `.kiro/` must be re-included, or it will not run on commits that touch only that file. The re-included runbook is the precedent and the inline comment says why.
  - Trade-off: pinning mtimes means the zips no longer record when they were built. Build provenance comes from the commit, not the archive, so nothing depended on it.
  - Verified locally, on this candidate: `npm run typecheck` clean; `npm run lint` clean; `npm run test:unit` **84 files / 1059 tests passing** (this matters specifically because `backend/src/__tests__/task10-3-retained-findings.test.ts` reads `.github/workflows/deploy-staging.yml` and asserts on it, and that file was edited here); `python -m unittest infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` **19 tests OK** (it reads the edited `transport.tf`); `node --test infra/tests/cds-kms.test.mjs` **6/6**; `test:cds-safety-regression --check`, `test:cds-resilience-campaign`, `cds:retirement-manifest --check`, and `generate-cds-contract-types.mjs --check` all exit 0. All four workflows parse as YAML, all seven jobs carry a numeric timeout, and no workflow still references the `lambda-dist-*` key or the `cache-hit` guard.
  - Terraform-verified after tooling became available: `terraform fmt -check -recursive infra` exits 0, so the hand-matched alignment in the three edited `.tf` files was correct; `terraform init -backend=false && terraform validate` succeeds for `dev`, `staging`, and `prod` with only the pre-existing `hash_key`/`range_key` deprecation warnings. `terraform providers lock` for all three environments took each lock file from 2 `h1:` hashes to 6 (two providers × three platforms), and its own output confirmed ADR-20260817-01's diagnosis exactly: `windows_amd64` checksums "were already tracked", while `linux_amd64` and `darwin_arm64` had to be obtained.
  - **`terraform plan` and `terraform apply` were deliberately NOT run, and this is a hard input blocker rather than a caution.** `infra/environments/dev/variables.tf` requires `seed_account_password` with no default, and `infra/modules/cognito/seed.tf` consumes it as both `password_version = sha256(var.seed_account_password)` and `SEED_PASSWORD` for the account-seeding provisioner. The real value lives only in the `DEV_SEED_ACCOUNT_PASSWORD` GitHub secret. Supplying any placeholder that satisfies the complexity validation would change the `sha256` trigger, re-run the provisioner, and **reset the seeded dev demo, role, and smoke Cognito account passwords to that placeholder** — desynchronising them from the secret the smoke tests use. A local apply is therefore not a lower-confidence version of the pipeline's apply; it is a different and destructive operation. `deploy-dev.yml` holds the real secret and is the correct executor, which is also the ordering ADR-20260817-01 deliberately established.
  - Also not run for the same reason: no `terraform plan` output exists for this candidate, so ADR-20260817-01 follow-up (a) and this ADR's follow-up (b) both remain open and must be satisfied from the CI deploy log rather than locally. No remote state was read, no state lock was acquired, and no `.terraform.tfstate.lock.info` was left behind.
  - **Still not verified.** The `paths-ignore` behaviour was validated against a local reimplementation of GitHub's documented evaluation rules, **not** against GitHub's parser, so the first docs-only and first mixed docs+code commits are what confirm it. Deterministic zips were verified with `bestzip`'s behaviour on this Windows machine; CI runs on `ubuntu-latest` where `bestzip` shells out to native `zip` instead, so the property must be re-confirmed from the first two CI runs (cross-machine byte equality is neither expected nor required — only stability across rebuilds on the same builder). The reinstated plugin cache has not executed on a runner; a local `TF_PLUGIN_CACHE_DIR` trial was attempted and abandoned because the provider downloads exceeded the available command window, so the first CI Terraform matrix is what proves it. Its failure mode is contained: the affected job runs `validate` only, holds no credentials, and touches no AWS resource.
  - Follow-up actions: (0) **done** — cross-platform provider lock hashes committed for `dev`, `staging`, and `prod`, and `TF_PLUGIN_CACHE_DIR` reinstated in the CI Terraform matrix, keyed per environment to avoid the three-way cache-save race. `infra/bootstrap/.terraform.lock.hcl` was **not** relocked: CI never initialises that directory, so it carries no CI benefit, and it is a one-time remote-state setup holding local state. Worth doing opportunistically for local Apple Silicon use, not as part of this pass. The matrix-collapse trade-off is still undecided and still recorded under Alternatives. (a) ~~**ADR-20260817-01's follow-up (a) remains open and is a prerequisite for any cost claim about Amplify: `enable_auto_build = false` is still unapplied, so every push may still be paying for two builds.**~~ **Superseded 2026-08-18 (ADR-20260818-01):** dev *is* applied. `aws amplify get-app --app-id d27420akzuajw` and `get-branch --branch-name main` both report auto-build `false`, and `list-apps` returns only `bayanhealth-dev-frontend`, so staging and prod have no Amplify app to apply to. Dev is no longer paying for two builds per push. This ADR was written before that verification was possible and its statement was correct at the time of writing. (b) The first dev deploy must produce a plan and it must be read before the apply proceeds — it will contain this ADR's `audit_reconciliation_schedule_expression` change, ADR-20260817-01's `enable_auto_build` flip and `custom_rule` removal, and a one-time `source_code_hash` change on all 21 Lambda functions as the deterministic zips replace the mtime-varying ones. That the 21 updates appear **once and not again** is the actual acceptance criterion for the determinism change. (c) ADR-20260817-01 follow-up (c) is now more urgent, not less: the `custom_rule` removal changes 404 behaviour and will land in the same apply as everything here, so it needs its own confirmation on dev. (d) confirm the first docs-only commit skips CI and the first mixed docs+code commit does not. (e) confirm the plugin cache hits on the second CI run rather than failing checksum verification, which is the specific failure ADR-20260817-01 predicted. (f) consider whether CI's `next build` should exist at all given Amplify rebuilds the same output — left in place here because it gates `manifest:cds --check`.
- Notes: The five CDS alarms previously stuck in `ALARM` are already addressed in Terraform by `local.idle_tolerant_missing_data` (ADR-20260804-02) and were not touched. `infra/modules/http_api/monitoring.tf`'s hard `treat_missing_data = "breaching"` alarm was checked and is gated on `local.monitoring_enabled = var.sns_alarm_topic_arn != ""`, which dev does not set, so it is not created there. Separately and outside this pass: no `aws_guardduty_detector`, `aws_config_configuration_recorder`, or `aws_cloudtrail` trail exists anywhere in `infra/`, despite ADR-20260710-01 describing a GuardDuty and AWS Config baseline — if those services are enabled they were enabled out of band, which is both an ADR-20260514-03 deviation and an unbudgeted line item. Recorded here as a finding only; investigating it is separate work.
- Related files: `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.github/workflows/deploy-dev.yml`, `.github/workflows/deploy-staging.yml`, `.github/workflows/destroy-staging.yml`, `backend/scripts/package-lambdas.mjs`, `infra/modules/cds_operations/variables.tf`, `infra/modules/cds_operations/transport.tf`, `infra/environments/dev/main.tf`, `infra/environments/dev/.terraform.lock.hcl`, `infra/environments/staging/.terraform.lock.hcl`, `infra/environments/prod/.terraform.lock.hcl`, `infra/modules/notifications/variables.tf` (read-only), `infra/modules/cognito/seed.tf` (read-only), `infra/environments/dev/variables.tf` (read-only), `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` (read-only), `backend/src/__tests__/task10-3-retained-findings.test.ts` (read-only), `amplify.yml` (read-only)
---

### ADR-20260818-01: The frontend diff gate could never skip a build, and `--commit-id` never pinned one

- Date: 2026-08-18
- Status: Accepted — repository-side implementation complete and locally validated against live read-only AWS data. No environment mutation performed. Not qualified.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: A post-implementation audit of ADR-20260817-01 against the live dev app found that two of its four changes did not do what the ADR states. Both were invisible from the repository alone, and one of them silently cancelled the largest projected saving. ADR-20260817-01 had already listed `start-job --commit-id` honouring an explicit SHA as an unverified assumption requiring a real dev run; this ADR resolves it, and finds the answer is no.

  1. **The diff gate matched zero jobs, so it failed open on every run and never skipped a build.** The gate selected its diff anchor with `select(.status == "SUCCEED" and .jobType == "RELEASE")`. **`ListJobs` does not return `jobType` at all.** Read-only verification against `d27420akzuajw`: every `JobSummary` in a 50-item response carries exactly `jobArn`, `jobId`, `commitId`, `commitTime`, `startTime`, `status`, `endTime`, and the set of distinct `jobType` values across all of them is `[null]`. `GetJob`'s `job.summary` omits it too, so there is no drop-in substitute. The predicate was therefore unsatisfiable: `LAST_SHA` was always empty, the gate always reached `open_gate "no previously succeeded RELEASE job"`, and the frontend rebuilt on every deploy exactly as before the ADR. The failure mode was self-concealing — the run log reported a deliberate-looking fail-open rather than a bug — and it cost up to five paginated `list-jobs` calls per deploy to reach a foregone conclusion.

  2. **`--commit-id` does not pin which commit Amplify builds.** The StartJob API reference states that job type `RELEASE` starts a job with the latest change on the specified branch; `commitId` is recorded metadata from the repository provider, and is only *required* for `RETRY`. Amplify checks out the branch tip regardless. So ADR-20260817-01's decision to "deploy `github.event.workflow_run.head_sha`, not `github.sha`" holds for the checkout, Terraform, and the Lambda artifacts, but **not for the frontend**: if `main` advances while CI runs, Amplify builds the newer tip and the deployed frontend is a commit no CI run validated. That is the same failure class `workflow_run` gating was introduced to close, left open on the frontend half. The dev job history corroborates the mechanism: jobs 129–136 sit in pairs roughly four minutes apart, one recording a real SHA and one recording the literal string `"HEAD"` — the retired webhook build versus the old CLI `start-job` that passed no `--commit-id`.

  A third, narrower gap: the gate keyed solely on watched source paths, but the frontend inlines `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` at build time from Amplify app environment variables that Terraform derives from the API Gateway and Cognito outputs. A backend-only commit that replaced the API or the user pool would change what the frontend must be built with while touching no watched path, so the gate would skip the build and leave a deployed bundle pointing at a dead endpoint — a stale-deploy hazard created by the gate itself.

- Decision:
  - **Drop the `jobType` predicate; scope by branch instead.** `--branch-name` already restricts results to builds of that branch, which is what the predicate was reaching for. Verified against the captured live response: the old filter yields empty, the new one resolves `f7fb6078fa57f81e538fc87dee25035981051a43` (job 140, the newest succeeded build).
  - **Require the anchor to be a 40-hex SHA and page past anything else.** A `start-job` without `--commit-id` records the literal `"HEAD"`, and the dev history contains several. Such an entry is useless as a diff anchor and would only fail open one step later at `git cat-file`, so the selector now skips to the newest usable commit. Confirmed that `"HEAD"` is the only non-SHA value present.
  - **Pass the branch tip as `--commit-id`, not `DEPLOY_SHA`.** Since the value is recorded rather than honoured, and since the gate reads it back as "the commit whose frontend is deployed", recording a commit Amplify did not build would anchor every future diff to the wrong tree. Omitting the flag is not an option — that reintroduces `"HEAD"`. The tip is resolved from `refs/remotes/origin/<branch>`, falling back to `DEPLOY_SHA` with a notice.
  - **Surface the tip/`DEPLOY_SHA` divergence as a warning rather than closing it.** When they differ, the run now states plainly that the frontend about to deploy is a commit this run did not validate while the backend artifacts are from the validated one. Closing it properly requires `job-type MANUAL` with an uploaded source archive, which replaces Amplify's Git integration for the frontend and is out of scope here. Failing the run instead was rejected: it would block a legitimate deploy over a condition the operator cannot fix from the workflow.
  - **Gate on build inputs, not just paths.** `aws amplify get-app --query 'app.environmentVariables'` is snapshotted *before* `terraform apply`, filtered to `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`, canonicalised with `jq -cS`, and compared after. Any difference forces a build and logs the changed keys. Chosen over widening the watched-path list to include `infra/`, which would be brittle in both directions — it would trigger on infra changes that cannot affect the bundle, and still miss a value changing for a reason not visible as a path edit. An unreadable snapshot (no app in state yet, or a failed call) is recorded as `UNKNOWN` and fails open, consistent with the gate's existing asymmetry.
  - **Split `terraform init` into its own step** in both deploy workflows, because the pre-apply snapshot needs `terraform output` before `apply` runs.
  - Smaller corrections in the same pass: `NEXT_PUBLIC_WS_URL` added to CI's frontend build, which set only four of the five variables `validate-env.mjs` requires and so was gating on a bundle that inlined the WebSocket URL as `undefined`; `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/package-lock.json` removed from `deploy-dev.yml`'s `cache-dependency-path`, since that workflow no longer installs frontend dependencies and the entry only churned the backend cache key; `force_build` dispatch input added to `deploy-dev.yml` for parity with staging, so a wrongly skipped build now has an escape hatch in the environment where the gate actually runs; the dead `outputs.should_build` job output removed from both deploy jobs; and `destroy-staging.yml` changed from `plan -destroy` followed by a separate `destroy` to `plan -destroy -out` followed by `apply`, so the preview is what executes rather than a second independent refresh whose result may differ from the one reviewed.
- Alternatives considered:
  - **Call `get-job` per candidate to recover `jobType`.** Rejected: `GetJob`'s `job.summary` omits the field as well, verified against job 140, so this trades N extra API calls for the same missing data.
  - **Store the last-deployed SHA in the repository or in SSM instead of reading it back from Amplify.** A legitimate design that removes the dependency on Amplify's job metadata entirely. Rejected for now because it introduces a second source of truth that can desynchronise from what Amplify actually deployed, and because a write to SSM is an environment mutation requiring its own point-of-action authorization. Recorded as follow-up if the metadata proves unreliable.
  - **Fail the deploy when the branch tip differs from the CI-validated SHA.** Rejected as above: it converts a visible, benign race into a hard stop that the operator can only clear by re-running.
  - **Widen `WATCHED` to include `infra/`.** Rejected in favour of comparing the resolved environment variables, which is what actually gets inlined.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the gate can now skip a build. Verified end-to-end with the gate step extracted verbatim from `deploy-dev.yml` and run against the real captured `list-jobs` response with `aws` stubbed: it emits `should_build=false` for an unchanged tree, `should_build=true` with the changed key named on `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` drift, and fails open for both an `UNKNOWN` snapshot and an operator `force_build`. This is the first time the step has been observed producing `false`.
  - Positive: the stale-deploy hazard the gate would otherwise have introduced is closed before it could fire, rather than after an incident.
  - Positive: the frontend/backend commit divergence is now stated in the run log instead of being contradicted by it.
  - Negative — **the savings are small in absolute terms, and this should be said plainly.** Measured from the live dev job history, builds 137–140 ran 2m45s–3m21s. At $0.01 per standard build minute that is roughly $0.03 per build, so skipping the ~45 of 126 monthly commits that touch nothing under the watched paths saves on the order of **$1.35/month** in build minutes. The durable benefits are the recurring CDN storage that no longer accumulates per redundant build (billed at $0.023/GB-month and, per the Amplify pricing page, recurring until the app is deleted), shorter pipeline latency, and correctness. ADR-20260817-01's framing invited an expectation of larger savings than the pipeline can deliver; the remaining money is in runtime, not builds.
  - Neutral: `.next/cache` acceptance of the `'!cache/**/*'` artifact exclusion remains unverified. The [buildspec reference](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amplify/latest/userguide/yml-specification-syntax.html) documents `artifacts>files` only as a list of files to deploy with `**/*` for everything, and says nothing about negation, so it may be honoured or silently ignored. Carried forward from ADR-20260817-01 follow-up (b).
- Validation performed:
  - All four workflows parse as YAML and pass `@action-validator/cli`.
  - All 52 `run` blocks across the four workflows parse under `bash -n` with GitHub expressions substituted (0 syntax errors).
  - `jq` filters exercised against the live captured `ListJobs` and `GetApp` responses: old anchor filter empty, new filter resolves job 140's SHA, `"HEAD"` confirmed as the only non-SHA `commitId`, changed-key diff output correct.
  - Gate step simulated across four scenarios as described above. Harness was throwaway and removed; `git status` confirms no stray files.
  - Not run: `terraform plan`, any `terraform apply`, and any real workflow execution. AWS access used in this task was read-only (`sts get-caller-identity`, `amplify get-app`/`get-branch`/`list-apps`/`list-jobs`/`get-job`).
- Amendment 1 (2026-08-18): **the dev plan was unauditable exactly when it mattered most.** `deploy-dev.yml` piped `terraform plan` through `tail -100`, which discards the *beginning* of every plan — the header, the summary position, and the first resources. ADR-20260817-02's acceptance criterion for the determinism change is that the 21 Lambda `source_code_hash` updates "appear once and not again", which can only be read off a plan. Measured against a faithful 21-Lambda plan: **129 lines**, so `tail -100` would have cut the first 29 including `Terraform will perform the following actions:` and several resource blocks. The one plan that had to be audited was the one guaranteed to be truncated.
  - `plan` and `apply` are now separate steps. Plan writes full output to `plan.txt`, echoes all of it inside a collapsed `::group::`, and uploads it as a `plan-dev-<run_id>-<attempt>` artifact with 30-day retention. Upload is `if: always()` and sits *before* apply, so the plan survives a failed apply. `apply` consumes the same `tfplan` file the plan produced, so what was reviewed is what executes.
  - `-detailed-exitcode` is used so the step can distinguish "no changes" (0) from "changes present" (2) from "plan failed" (1), publishing `has_changes` for later steps and failing loudly only on 1. The `Plan:` summary line is written to `$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY` so the change count is visible without expanding the log.
  - Verified by extracting the step verbatim and running it against a stubbed `terraform` across all three exit codes: no-changes gives exit 0 / `has_changes=false` / notice; changes gives exit 0 / `has_changes=true` / 129 lines captured / correct summary line; error gives exit 1 with an `::error::` annotation. Harness was throwaway and removed.
  - `deploy-staging.yml` is deliberately **not** given the same treatment here. It is worse — `terraform apply -auto-approve` with no plan step at all — but it is being restructured wholesale by the staging/prod promotion roadmap (`architecture/CICD_PROMOTION_ROADMAP.md`), and a half-measure that the roadmap immediately replaces is waste. Recorded there as a required change, not deferred indefinitely.
- Amendment 1 outcome, measured on the real run (`32132161079`, `bc1e14d`): the dev plan is **709 lines**. `tail -100` was discarding **609 of them — 86% of the plan**, including the entire refresh phase and most resource blocks. The estimate in Amendment 1 (129 lines, from a synthetic 21-Lambda plan) understated it by more than 5×. The uploaded artifact captured all 709 lines and the plan reads exactly as ADR-20260817-02 predicted: `Plan: 0 to add, 22 to change, 0 to destroy` — 21 `aws_lambda_function` `source_code_hash` updates as the deterministic zips land, plus `module.cds_operations.aws_cloudwatch_event_rule.audit_reconciliation` for the `rate(1 minute)` → `rate(5 minutes)` change. Nothing created, nothing destroyed. **ADR-20260817-02 follow-up (b) is now half-satisfied**: the plan has been produced and read. The remaining half — that the 21 updates appear *once and not again* — is confirmed by the next successful apply followed by a clean plan.
- Amendment 1 defect, found by that same run and fixed in the follow-up commit: the plan step **failed with exit code 2** the first time it ran. GitHub invokes `run` blocks with `bash -e`, which a subsequent `set -uo pipefail` does not clear, so `terraform plan -detailed-exitcode` returning 2 ("changes present" — the expected case) aborted the step before `rc=$?` could be read. The local harness had run the extracted step under plain `bash`, so it reproduced neither the flag nor the failure. Both plan steps and both apply steps now capture status with `|| rc=$?`, which `-e` tolerates, and the harness was corrected to invoke `bash -e`. Re-verified across all three exit codes under the correct flags. The lesson generalises: a step harness that does not replicate the runner's shell flags is not a test of the step.
- Amendment 2 (2026-08-18): **symmetrical per-job gating and out-of-band drift detection.**
  - `ci.yml` gains a `changes` job (`dorny/paths-filter`, SHA-pinned) publishing `backend`/`frontend`/`infra`/`contract` outputs, with each job gated on the areas that can actually affect it. The filters are derived from what each check *reads*, not from directory names, and that turned out to matter: `backend/src/__tests__/task10-3-security-scan.test.ts` and `task10-3-retained-findings.test.ts` assert on `.github/workflows/deploy-staging.yml` and four files under `infra/`, and `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` reads `contracts/openapi.yaml` and `architecture/CDS_SECURITY_OPERATIONS.md`. The backend job therefore must trigger on `infra/**` and `.github/workflows/**`, so it still runs for most commits. The honest saving is narrower than "only relevant builds" implies: a frontend-only commit skips the backend suite and all three Terraform legs, and a backend-only commit skips the frontend build, which is the slowest job. Widening it further requires decoupling the Task 10.3 scanners from live infra files, which is a change to a security regression's inputs rather than a CI tweak, and is recorded as follow-up.
  - `deploy-dev.yml` gains an `infra-gate` step so a commit touching no Terraform input skips plan and apply entirely. `terraform init` stays unconditional because the seeded-account check, the smoke test, and the Amplify gate all read `terraform output` from existing state. The anchor is this workflow's newest successful run whose head SHA resolves locally and is an ancestor of `HEAD`, for the same reason the Amplify gate anchors on last-deployed rather than `HEAD~1`: a run of frontend-only commits must not let an infra change fall outside a one-commit window. Fails open in every ambiguous case. Verified against real repo history with `gh` stubbed across six scenarios, including a deliberate `tr -d '[:space:]'` guard — a stray carriage return makes every SHA 41 characters, `git cat-file -e` fails on all of them, and the gate degrades to permanent fail-open that looks exactly like working correctly. That is the same self-concealing shape as the `jobType` bug, so it is defended against rather than assumed away.
  - `drift-dev.yml` is new: a daily `21:00 UTC` (05:00 Asia/Manila) plan against real dev state, sharing `deploy-dev`'s concurrency group so it can never plan while an apply is in flight. It **plans and reports; it does not apply on a schedule.** An unattended `apply -auto-approve` would push whatever sits on `main` into dev with nobody reading a plan, which directly contradicts the auditability Amendment 1 just established — detection is what the gate needs, reconciliation is a decision. Drift fails the run deliberately so it cannot sit green and unnoticed, and `workflow_dispatch` with `apply: true` reconciles the reviewed plan. `npm run package:lambdas` runs before the plan and is mandatory, not incidental: without it `fileexists(built) ? built : lambda_placeholder.zip` reports all 21 functions reverting to the placeholder, i.e. drift that does not exist. **Known risk:** deterministic zips are stable per builder, not across builders (ADR-20260817-02), so if the `ubuntu-latest` image changes between a deploy and a drift run the plan may show all 21 hashes differing. Treat the first week of drift runs as calibration, and if that fires, the fix is to compare against a recorded hash manifest rather than to loosen the check.
- **Production verification (2026-08-18). Everything below is observed in real runs, not inferred.**
  - **The gate resolves a real anchor for the first time.** Run `32133717810` logged `Frontend build ENABLED: f7fb607 -> 2f2cce1 touches watched paths`. The immediately preceding run under the old code, `32025468615`, logged `Frontend build ENABLED (fail-open): no previously succeeded RELEASE job for main; nothing to diff against`. That pair is the before/after proof that the `jobType` predicate was unsatisfiable and is now fixed — the gate had failed open on every run since ADR-20260817-01 shipped it.
  - **The infra gate works and anchors correctly.** Same run: `Terraform WILL run: f7fb607 -> 2f2cce1 touches backend infra contracts`. It correctly anchored on `f7fb607`, the last *successful* deploy, skipping the failed `bc1e14d` run in between — which is precisely the behaviour the "newest successful run that is an ancestor of HEAD" rule exists to produce.
  - **Per-job gating works.** CI run `32133570020` on a workflows-and-docs commit: `Detect Changed Areas`, `Backend Build`, and all three `Terraform Validate` legs ran; `Frontend Build` and `OpenAPI Validate` were **skipped**. The frontend build is the slowest job in the workflow.
  - **ADR-20260817-02 follow-up (b) is now CLOSED, both halves.** The apply in run `32133717810` succeeded, and a subsequent independent plan (drift run `32134501090`, a different runner invocation) reported **`No changes. Your infrastructure matches the configuration.`** The 21 `source_code_hash` updates appeared **once and not again**, which was the stated acceptance criterion for the determinism change. `aws events list-rules` confirms `bayanhealth-dev-cds-audit-reconciliation` is now `rate(5 minutes)`.
  - **The cross-builder determinism risk did not materialise.** The concern recorded in Amendment 2 — that `bestzip` output might differ between runner invocations and produce false drift — was not borne out: two separate `ubuntu-latest` invocations produced byte-identical zips. This is now observed once, not proven in general; a runner image change remains the scenario to watch.
  - **`drift-dev.yml` works end-to-end**: plan clean, `Terraform apply` skipped (no `apply: true`), the deliberate drift-failure step skipped, plan artifact uploaded. The mandatory `package:lambdas` step did its job — had it been absent, the plan would have reported 21 functions reverting to `lambda_placeholder.zip`.
- Reconciliation with ADR-20260817-02 (rebased onto it, 2026-08-18): the two passes were written in parallel against the same `f7fb607` base and overlap in five files. Where they disagree, **ADR-20260817-02 wins**, because its positions are measured rather than inferred:
  - **`timeout-minutes`**: both passes added limits to every job. ADR-20260817-02's values are kept (CI backend 15, frontend 20, contract 10, terraform 20; dev 45, staging 45, destroy 60) over this ADR's rounder guesses. This ADR's separate `timeout-minutes` work is therefore dropped as duplicate, not reversed.
  - **The `backend/dist` cache**: this ADR retained it and described `ci.yml` as its sole writer. ADR-20260817-02 deletes it outright, having measured 46.0 MB moved to avoid 1.7s of esbuild *and* identified a correctness hole where a partial restore satisfied the `cache-hit` guard and let Terraform's `fileexists(...) ? ... : placeholder_zip` fallback ship placeholder handler code behind a green run. That is the stronger argument and it is adopted; nothing in this ADR depends on the cache existing.
  - **Path filtering**: this ADR recorded it as a recommendation. ADR-20260817-02 implemented it, and additionally found that `architecture/CDS_SECURITY_OPERATIONS.md` is an input to `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` rather than an inert document — a dependency this pass would have missed. Its implementation is kept as the base for the per-job work that follows.
  - Non-overlapping items from this ADR are unaffected: the gate fix, the `--commit-id` correction, build-input gating, `NEXT_PUBLIC_WS_URL`, the `cache-dependency-path` trim, dev's `force_build` input, the dead job output removal, `destroy-staging`'s plan-file apply, and `.npm-cache` in `.gitignore`.
- Follow-up actions: (a) on the first dev deploy after this lands, confirm the gate logs a real `LAST_SHA` rather than the fail-open notice, and confirm whether the tip/`DEPLOY_SHA` warning fires; (b) ADR-20260817-01's follow-ups (b) through (f) and ADR-20260817-02's follow-ups (b) through (f) remain open — note that both ADRs' follow-up (a) regarding `enable_auto_build` is now **closed**, since dev is verified applied; (c) **`paths-ignore` negation needs verification, and it is the same class of risk as the `jobType` bug this ADR fixes.** ADR-20260817-02 re-includes `architecture/CDS_SECURITY_OPERATIONS.md` with a `!` pattern *inside* `paths-ignore`. GitHub's guidance is that `paths-ignore` is for pure exclusion and that `paths` with `!` is the mechanism when both inclusion and exclusion are needed; negation inside a `*-ignore` filter is not documented. ADR-20260817-02 acknowledges it validated this against a local reimplementation of the documented rules rather than GitHub's parser. If the negation is not honoured, a commit editing only that runbook skips CI entirely and the test asserting on its contents never runs — precisely the hazard the re-inclusion exists to prevent. Verify with a commit touching only that file, or remove the dependency by having the test read a fixture instead of a runbook. Note the asymmetry that makes `paths-ignore` the right base choice regardless: it fails safe by running too often, whereas a `paths` allowlist fails unsafe by skipping.
- Related files: `.github/workflows/deploy-dev.yml`, `.github/workflows/deploy-staging.yml`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.github/workflows/destroy-staging.yml`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/.gitignore`, `architecture/DECISIONS.md`

### ADR-20260819-01: Consultation media layer (phase one — live video), superseding the Google Meet integration path and retiring its demo stand-in

- Date: 2026-08-19
- Status: Accepted — repository-side implementation complete and locally validated. `dev` Terraform is defined, not applied. Not qualified in any environment.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: `architecture/TELECONSULT_VIDEO_AND_AI_SCRIBE.md` recorded the engineering position that Google Meet is not the video path — every blocker (Workspace admin access, organizer identity model, audio capture for a future scribe) sits outside engineering's control, while a provider-adapter seam is roughly 70% vendor-independent and makes the vendor choice a two-week change. ADR-20260807-06 had already accepted a manually provisioned, single-shared-room video link (`DEMO_VIDEO_JOIN_URL`) as an explicit, disclosed stand-in for the gated Meet integration, to be replaced once the real seam existed. This ADR records that replacement: the `.kiro/specs/consultation-media-layer/` specification (requirements.md, design.md, tasks.md) implements phase one — live video — of a **media layer**, deliberately framed so that consented recording, transcription, and an eventual AI scribe are configuration changes and new producers rather than a data migration. Full design rationale lives in `design.md`; this entry summarizes it at governance density.
- Decision:
  - **Media-layer framing; Daily is the launch provider.** Every entity, key shape, and retention class is chosen so a future producer (recording, transcription) needs no schema change. `lib/video-provider.ts` is the only module that talks to a video vendor, selected by `VIDEO_PROVIDER` (`none` | `daily`), routed through the existing PayRex-pattern `circuit-breaker.ts`, degrading the media path to `unavailable` on any failure while the existing WebSocket chat/presence/typing channel remains the retained clinical communication path untouched.
  - **Supersedes ADR-20260720-02 as the video path.** Google Meet is retired as the MVP video-consultation provider. ADR-20260720-02's authorization, disclosure, and system-of-record rules are **preserved in substance**: BayanHealth remains the system of record and authorization boundary for booking assignment, consent, session eligibility, clinical completion, document release, chat fallback, and audit; only the backend calls the provider; the browser never receives a long-lived provider credential, only a participant-scoped, time-limited Join_Credential (`token`) minted fresh per request and never persisted; no recording, transcription, or provider add-on ships in this phase.
  - **Retires the demo stand-in accepted under ADR-20260807-06.** `DEMO_VIDEO_JOIN_URL`, `lib/video-session.ts`, its test, the `videoJoinUrl` booking-response field, and its four frontend consumers are deleted (repository implementation tasks 10.1–10.2, 11.2). The single-shared-room, no-lifecycle, Google-controlled-access limitations that ADR-20260807-06 explicitly flagged no longer apply once this specification's routes are enabled.
  - **Three contracted operations** join the existing `bookings` handler's `routeKey` switch (design decision D1): `POST /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/video-session` (idempotent room creation and credential mint, at-most-one-room-per-Booking via a DynamoDB conditional write that is won *before* any provider call — D2, D3), `GET /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/video-session` (safe, side-effect-free), and `POST /v1/bookings/{bookingId}/video-session/end`. Lifecycle cleanup on consultation completion, cancellation, and reschedule runs as a shared, best-effort `lib/video-session-cleanup.ts` function called synchronously from whichever handler serves each operation — `payments` for completion, `bookings` for cancellation and reschedule (D4, and Finding F1/F2 below).
  - **Definition-only entity shapes, ship with no writer.** `lib/media-artifact.ts` (Media_Artifact), `lib/consent-record.ts` (Consent_Record), and `lib/transcript.ts` (Transcript_Artifact, a Media_Artifact kind) are added to `lib/dynamo.ts`'s key registry and to `architecture/DATA_MODEL.md`, each mechanically verified to import no `docClient` and no `@aws-sdk/lib-dynamodb` command, so the zero-write guarantee is checkable rather than asserted. Two new TTL constants are introduced for these two new entity classes — a **365-day** training-eligible retention class and a **5-year** Consent_Record retention — matching existing magnitudes already used elsewhere (`Ttl.SolverContent`/`PatientEducation`/`DrugData`/`Slot` at one year; `Ttl.PatientProfile` at five years). **No existing retention duration changed.** `Video_Session` itself reuses the existing `Ttl.Session` duration under a new label, because no existing `RetentionClass` label described a media-layer session and mislabelling it as CDS-target data was rejected (Finding F4).
  - **Requirement 25 media-object-storage decisions are recorded, not provisioned.** This specification decides — and records in `architecture/DATA_MODEL.md` — a dedicated Media_Layer S3 bucket (separate from `ai_raw_logs` and `media_private`) with its own dedicated KMS key; object lifecycle expiration derived from the referencing artifact's retention class rather than configured independently (closing a real drift hazard already present in `media_private`, whose `media_expiration_days` is 365 by module default but 90 in `dev`); identical retention across `dev`/`staging`/`prod`; versioning enabled and Object Lock deliberately **not** enabled, because Object Lock would make Data Privacy Act erasure impossible on the store most likely to receive an erasure request; `s3:PutObject` restricted to the write path with read granted only to a separate curation role and to no consultation-serving execution role; all four public-access blocks on with server-side encryption enforced; and an object key layout free of names and PHI. **No bucket, no KMS key, and no lifecycle rule is provisioned under this specification** — nothing here writes a media object, so provisioning is explicitly left to a future recording specification (Requirement 25 criterion 10).
  - **Design decisions D1–D11** (full rationale in `design.md`, "Design Decisions and Rationale"): D1 keeps the three routes in `bookings.ts` rather than a new handler; D2 derives the provider room identifier locally (`"bh-" + environment + "-" + sha256(bookingId + "#" + roomEpoch)`) with a `roomEpoch` counter so a re-opened room cannot be re-entered on a credential minted before the doctor closed it; D3 records `roomCreatedAt` to close a crash window between the conditional write and the provider call; D4 runs lifecycle cleanup as a shared lib function rather than a cross-handler call; D5 returns `404` (not `400`) for a malformed `bookingId`, favoring concealment totality; D6 names the credential response field `token` so the existing `sanitizeForIdempotencyStorage` redacts it with no code change; D7 emits metrics as CloudWatch Embedded Metric Format log records rather than adding the `@aws-sdk/client-cloudwatch` dependency; D8 splits audit durability — disclosure-path events fail-closed, cleanup/denial events best-effort; D9 accepts that a provider failure burns a mint-ceiling slot rather than risking an unbounded under-count from a compensating decrement; D10 reuses the opaque per-participant provider label as the future Media_Artifact track identity; D11 states where property-based testing does and does not apply, and explicitly excludes Requirement 25 because it provisions nothing.
  - **Findings F1–F4 and two wording notes**, each with its recommended amendment (full evidence in `design.md`, "Findings Requiring a Requirements Amendment"): **F1** — consultation completion is served by `payments.ts`, not `bookings.ts`; the requirement should say "the handler serving each lifecycle operation." **F2** — a reschedule surface (`PUT /v1/bookings/{bookingId}` with `scheduledAt`) exists and should be named rather than treated as conditional. **F3** — the "single execution role" framing already describes reality: `aws_iam_role.lambda_exec` is shared by every non-admin handler including `bookings`/`payments`/`media`/`chat`/`intake`/`drugs` and more, so granting the video credential and metric namespace on it (matching the existing PayRex precedent) satisfies the letter of the criteria while the intended blast-radius containment is not achieved; the requirement should name the shared role's actual scope, and a per-handler execution-role split is recorded as follow-up rather than done here. **F4** — no existing `RetentionClass` label fits a media-layer session, so `Ttl.VideoSession = Ttl.Session` under a new label is adopted; the requirement should be narrowed to "no new retention *duration*." **Wording note 1** — "byte-identical apart from `requestId`" is unachievable because `errorResponse` also stamps a `timestamp`; both fields should be named as elided. **Wording note 2** — the fixed evaluation order omits two reachable stages (`400 INVALID_PAYLOAD` for a missing/malformed `Idempotency-Key`, `409 IDEMPOTENCY_CONFLICT` on replay), both placed after the participant-relation check in the implementation so concealment holds; the order should name them.
  - **Evidence boundary, stated explicitly.** Repository implementation and a `dev` Terraform *definition* (not yet applied) are not staging qualification. Staging qualification is not canary authorization. Neither is production enablement or general availability. This ADR records design and implementation decisions only.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Build a receive-only WebRTC bot to capture audio from Google Meet for the future scribe: rejected — the only stack compute is Lambda, and every other candidate vendor needs no such bot.
  - Keep the ADR-20260807-06 stand-in indefinitely: rejected — one shared room with no lifecycle and provider-controlled access is acceptable for a single scripted demo and unacceptable with real patients, which is exactly why that ADR scoped it to `dev` only.
  - Reuse an existing CDS-target `RetentionClass` label for Video_Session rather than adding one: rejected — it would mislabel session data as CDS candidate data (Finding F4).
  - Split per-handler Lambda execution roles now, as part of this change: rejected for scope — real security work that benefits PayRex and CDS secrets at least as much as video, and belongs in its own change set with its own ADR (Finding F3).
  - Add `@aws-sdk/client-cloudwatch` and call `PutMetricData` directly: rejected — Requirement 24 criterion 10 forbids a new dependency, and Embedded Metric Format reaches the same CloudWatch metrics with no SDK call (D7).
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the vendor is now genuinely swappable behind one seam, with no product surface aware of Daily specifics beyond `<ConsultationVideo />`; a provider outage degrades to chat rather than failing the consultation; the demo stand-in's single-shared-room and no-lifecycle limitations are gone.
  - Positive: the definition-only entity shapes and the two new TTL constants mean a future recording specification adds producers and configuration, not a migration; the Requirement 25 object-store decisions are inherited rather than relitigated under delivery pressure.
  - Trade-off: the shared `lambda_exec` execution role's blast radius is unchanged and now also carries the video credential and metric namespace grants (Finding F3); this is consistent with the existing PayRex precedent but is recorded plainly rather than presented as containment.
  - Trade-off: EMF metrics mean the `cloudwatch:PutMetricData` grant added to `lambda_cloudwatch_metrics` is not load-bearing today (D7); it is in place only so a later switch to the SDK needs no IAM change.
  - Trade-off: four requirements statements (F1–F4) and two wording notes do not literally hold against the repository and need the amendments recorded above before the specification's acceptance criteria can be called accurate.
- Validation performed: backend typecheck, lint, and unit/property test suite; frontend typecheck, lint, and test suite; contract lint and generated-type drift check; `infra/tests/test_cds_infrastructure.py` route/contract parity; `terraform validate` and `fmt -check` across `dev`/`staging`/`prod`; the repository scan asserting the demo stand-in, `Google Meet`/`meet.google.com`, and `videoJoinUrl` are absent from backend, frontend, contract, and `infra/` sources (task 10.3). Not run: any `terraform apply` to any environment, any staging exercise, any canary observation.
- Follow-up actions: (a) the CloudWatch alarms and dashboard under the video metric namespace, deferred as fast-follow because Requirement 2's chat degradation makes an unpaged outage acceptable short-term — land before the corpus phase begins; (b) the property-based test suite beyond the four highest-value properties (credential non-disclosure, concealment indistinguishability, idempotent creation/read purity, degradation totality); (c) the per-handler Lambda execution-role split flagged by Finding F3, real security work benefiting PayRex and CDS secrets as well as video; (d) normalize the PayRex CloudWatch namespace scoping alongside the newly added video namespace on `lambda_cloudwatch_metrics`, since both currently sit on the same shared-role condition list.
- Launch dependencies tracked outside this specification (Requirement 19 criteria 3, 7, 8; Requirement 22 criterion 7): staging qualification of the three `video-session` routes is a launch-blocking dependency, not an optional follow-up, because live video is a launch-blocking core production feature (`VIDEO_PROVIDER = daily` in `dev`, `staging`, and `prod`); the Video_Provider credential must be populated in an environment's Secrets Manager secret before that environment's `video-session` routes are treated as enabled — a placeholder credential in `staging` or `prod` is a launch blocker, not a degraded-but-acceptable state, matching the trap PayRex currently sits in; and, under the Philippine Data Privacy Act, the cross-border transfer position, the provider's sub-processor list, and the provider's deletion guarantee must be confirmed in writing before Daily carries a real patient consultation.
- Related files: `backend/src/lib/video-provider.ts`, `backend/src/lib/video-session-store.ts`, `backend/src/lib/video-session-cleanup.ts`, `backend/src/lib/video-audit.ts`, `backend/src/lib/media-artifact.ts`, `backend/src/lib/consent-record.ts`, `backend/src/lib/transcript.ts`, `backend/src/lib/transcript-cds-socket.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/bookings.ts`, `backend/src/handlers/payments.ts`, `backend/src/lib/dynamo.ts`, `backend/src/lib/secrets.ts`, `backend/src/lib/circuit-breaker.ts`, `backend/src/lib/response.ts`, `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/media/components/ConsultationVideo.tsx`, `infra/modules/data_stores/main.tf`, `infra/modules/http_api/main.tf`, `architecture/DATA_MODEL.md`, `.kiro/specs/consultation-media-layer/`

### ADR-20260823-01: Four CDS drill-down metric filters were 80% of published CloudWatch series and were read by nothing

- Date: 2026-08-23
- Decision ID: `ADR-20260823-01`
- Title: Gate the dimensioned CDS drill-down metric filters off by default; keep the aggregate series and the log-based drill-down path
- Status: Accepted — repository change complete, `terraform validate` passes on all three roots, 19 Python + 6 KMS infrastructure tests pass. **Not applied to `dev`.** Applying it destroys four live metric filters and requires its own point-of-action authorization.
- Owner: Dev A (sole engineer)
- Context: The first end-to-end infrastructure cost audit (`architecture/INFRASTRUCTURE_COSTS.md`, 2026-08-23) found CloudWatch to be the largest line on the AWS bill — measured at ₱771/month in `dev`, two-thirds of that environment's ₱1,169 total. Reading the live account rather than the code explained why. `aws cloudwatch list-metrics` returned **46 series in `BayanHealth/CDS/dev`** plus 6 in `BayanHealth/Video/dev`, against 38 metric *names* defined in `monitoring.tf`. The gap is dimension fan-out: CloudWatch bills USD 0.30 per series per month, and four filters carry dimensions.
  - `AuditOutboxBacklogCountByShard` and `AuditOutboxOldestAgeSecondsByShard` publish **16 series each** — one per audit-outbox shard — for **32 of the 46 series, 70% of the namespace**. They publish on every idle audit sweep, so `dev` pays for them with nobody using the platform.
  - `GateDecisionsByOutput` (3 live series) pairs a 16-value `outcome` enum with an 8-value `outputType` enum, a **128-series ceiling** that grows silently as new combinations occur under real traffic. `RedFlagEvaluationsByOutcome` (1 live series) has an 18-series ceiling. At full cardinality the four would bill 178 series per environment, ₱3,295/month.
  - Cross-referencing every alarm (`describe-alarms`, 20 in the account) and every widget in the `bayanhealth-dev-cds-safety` dashboard against the published names: **none of the four is read by anything.** The alarms watch the dimensionless aggregates `AuditOutboxBacklogCount`, `AuditOutboxOldestAgeSeconds`, `GateDecisions`, and `RedFlagEvaluations`, all of which are retained.
  - CloudWatch charged this account **nothing at all until 13 August 2026**, when the series count crossed the free allowance of 10. From 15 August the billed rate settled at a steady $0.4032/day. The cost was invisible for as long as it was small, which is why no earlier cost pass caught it.
- Decision:
  - **New module variable `cds_drilldown_metrics_enabled`, default `false`**, gating the four dimensioned `aws_cloudwatch_log_metric_filter` resources in `infra/modules/cds_operations/monitoring.tf` via `count`. Follows the module's existing flag convention (`cds_retired_routes_enabled`, `payrex_enabled`, `reminder_schedule_enabled`).
  - **A flag rather than deletion.** The breakdowns are legitimate incident-investigation aids; what is illegitimate is paying for them continuously while nothing reads them. A flag makes the cost/benefit explicit, keeps the decision reversible per environment, and lets an operator turn them on for the duration of an incident.
  - **No environment root sets it.** All three inherit `false`. Enabling it anywhere is a separate, costed decision.
  - **The six unconsumed dimensionless filters are deliberately retained** — `stale_postflight`, `async_admission`, `lease_lost`, `audit_delivery`, `outbox_dlq`, `migration_reconciliation`. Each is a single series, USD 1.80/month for all six, and unlike the dimensioned four they cannot fan out. Recorded in a comment above `cds_metric_filters` so the trade-off is visible rather than implicit. Deleting counters that might matter during a clinical-safety incident is a poor trade for ₱111/month.
- Alternatives considered:
  - **Delete the four filters outright.** Rejected: loses the option to re-enable during an incident for no additional saving, since a `count = 0` resource costs nothing.
  - **Narrow the dimension enums instead** (restrict `outcome` on `GateDecisionsByOutput` from 16 values to `allow`/`deny`/`error`). Rejected as the primary fix — it caps the ceiling at 24 series but still bills for data nothing reads. Retained as a follow-up *if* the flag is ever turned on.
  - **Reduce the audit-outbox shard count from 16.** Rejected: shard count is a throughput and contention decision on the audit path, not an observability one. Changing it to save metric cost would be optimising the wrong dimension.
  - **Move to Embedded Metric Format.** Rejected: EMF metrics are billed as custom metrics at the same USD 0.30 per series. It changes the emission mechanism, not the cost driver.
  - **Suppress metrics in `dev` only.** Rejected as insufficient — the same fan-out would reappear in `prod` at higher cardinality, where the ceiling matters more.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: `dev` CloudWatch falls from a measured **₱771 to ₱173/month**, taking that environment's AWS bill from **₱1,169 to ₱570** — a 51% reduction. The forecast `prod` saving is a further **₱666/month**, for **₱1,265/month (₱15,180/year)** once both environments run. The uncapped-cardinality risk is removed: no future `outcome` × `outputType` combination can silently add ₱18.51/month.
  - Positive: no observability is lost. The four filter patterns read the same JSON log lines that the retained aggregate filters consume, so the per-shard and per-outcome detail stays queryable in CloudWatch Logs Insights for the log group's retention window (30 days for both CDS groups) at USD 0.007 per GB scanned — ₱0.03 to scan the whole of `dev`'s 0.060 GB.
  - Positive: no clinical-safety alarm changes behaviour. All 20 alarms watch retained metrics; the alarm set is untouched.
  - Trade-offs: a per-shard breakdown now requires a Logs Insights query rather than a pre-built metric, which is slower to reach during an incident. Judged acceptable because no alarm or dashboard depended on it, so nobody was reaching for it in the first place.
  - Trade-offs: adds one module variable. Accepted as the cost of keeping the capability reversible.
  - Follow-up actions: (a) apply to `dev` under its own authorization and confirm `list-metrics` returns 10 CDS series; (b) if the flag is ever enabled, narrow the `outcome` enum on `GateDecisionsByOutput` first; (c) attach a CloudWatch spend alarm before `prod` enables AWS Config, whose volume is the least predictable line in the cost audit.
- Applied-state record (2026-08-24): **applied to `dev`** by `Deploy Dev` run `32708485126` on commit `ef74b7d`, which is the sanctioned executor because it holds `DEV_SEED_ACCOUNT_PASSWORD` (a local apply would re-trigger `password_version = sha256(var.seed_account_password)` and reset the seeded Cognito accounts — see ADR-20260817-02). Plan read before apply: `Plan: 1 to add, 0 to change, 4 to destroy`. The four destroys are exactly `gate_decisions_by_output[0]`, `red_flag_by_outcome[0]`, `outbox_backlog_by_shard[0]`, and `outbox_age_by_shard[0]`; `Apply complete! Resources: 1 added, 0 changed, 4 destroyed`. Verified read-only afterwards: `describe-metric-filters` on `/aws/lambda/bayanhealth-dev-cds` no longer lists `gate-decisions-by-output` or `red-flag-by-outcome`, `/aws/lambda/bayanhealth-dev-cds-audit-outbox` no longer lists the two by-shard filters, and every aggregate filter this ADR promised to retain is still present on both groups (`gate-decision`, `red-flag-total`, `outbox-backlog`, `outbox-age`), including the six deliberately-kept dimensionless ones. The alarm set was not touched.
  - The `1 to add` is unrelated pre-existing drift, not a side effect of this change: `module.http_api.aws_sns_topic_subscription.payrex_pagerduty_placeholder[0]`. See the correction below.
- **Correction to follow-up (a): the acceptance criterion as originally written is wrong and cannot be satisfied.** It said to "confirm `list-metrics` returns 10 CDS series". Immediately after the apply `list-metrics` still returns **46**, and still names `AuditOutboxBacklogCountByShard`, `AuditOutboxOldestAgeSecondsByShard`, `GateDecisionsByOutput`, and `RedFlagEvaluationsByOutcome`. That is correct CloudWatch behaviour rather than a failed apply: deleting a metric filter stops future *publication*, but already-published series remain queryable for up to 15 months and keep appearing in `list-metrics`, which is not a view of what is currently being published. Destroying a filter cannot retract a series. The criterion is therefore replaced by the two checks that are actually decidable: (i) the four filters no longer exist on either log group — **verified above**; and (ii) the four series receive no new datapoints, which decays out of `list-metrics` on CloudWatch's own schedule rather than on ours. The billing effect follows (ii), not the `list-metrics` count.
  - Worth recording because it changes how the saving should be confirmed: **do not read the saving off `list-metrics`.** Read it off the CloudWatch line on the next full invoice. Anyone re-checking this in the next fortnight and finding 46 series should not conclude the change failed to apply.
  - One measurement taken while verifying, which strengthens the original finding rather than weakening it: with the correct dimension name (`Shard`, capitalised and zero-padded — an earlier check using `shard`/`0` matched nothing and was invalid), `get-metric-statistics` shows the by-shard series had **no datapoints at all in the preceding 24 hours**. So dev was carrying these as billable series in the months they last published while they were not even actively reporting. The waste was slightly worse than the ADR's "publish on every idle sweep" framing implies.
- Discovered during this apply, unrelated and unresolved — **dev has a perpetual-drift loop**: `aws_sns_topic_subscription.payrex_pagerduty_placeholder[0]` (`infra/modules/http_api/monitoring.tf:516`) is an `email` subscription to a hardcoded personal address, commented in-code as "Temporary - replace with actual PagerDuty email". An email subscription stays `PendingConfirmation` until the recipient clicks the confirmation link, and AWS deletes unconfirmed subscriptions after roughly three days. So Terraform creates it, nobody confirms it, AWS removes it, and the next plan wants to create it again. This is what failed the scheduled `Drift Detection (Dev)` runs on 2026-08-22 (`32599253041`) and 2026-08-23 (`32667174282`) — both reported exactly `Plan: 1 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy` for this one resource, and both were genuine drift rather than false positives from the `package:lambdas`/`source_code_hash` hazard Amendment 2 of ADR-20260818-01 warns about. It matters beyond tidiness: `drift-dev.yml` fails deliberately so drift is visible, and a detector that cries wolf nightly is one that stops being read, which is the same self-concealing failure class as the `jobType` bug. It needs its own decision (confirm a real alerting endpoint, move to an HTTPS/PagerDuty integration, or gate the placeholder off) and is deliberately **not** fixed here: the endpoint is a product/ops choice, not a cost one, and `payrex_enabled` is `false` in every environment so the topic it subscribes to carries no traffic today.

### ADR-20260824-01: Patient-education register naming — Filipino identifier, Taglish display

- Date: 2026-08-24
- Status: Accepted
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: Patient education has exactly two registers — English and one other — and the second one has been misnamed. The `*_fil` columns hold deliberately code-switched Filipino carrying English clinical terms; this is not new to the V2 revision. V1 already wrote "Sundin lang ang medicines na nasa doctor-approved Plan o prescription", and V2's PE-001 rewrite reads "Sundin lang ang medicines na nasa plano o reseta ni Doc" — both keep English clinical nouns (`Plan`, `prescription`/`reseta`, `Doc`) embedded in Filipino sentence structure. The accurate name for that register is **Taglish**, not "Filipino". `architecture/PATIENT_EDUCATION_CORPUS_SCHEMA.md` has asked for a "Common Filipino/Taglish name" in `condition_name_fil` since the first V1 ingest, so the naming ambiguity predates this ADR and this is a naming correction over existing content, not new content.
  Three unrelated `taglish` sightings elsewhere in the codebase are cited here so a reader does not confuse them with this corpus: `validLang = ['english', 'taglish']` in `backend/src/handlers/cds.ts`, which validates a request field on the retired patient-cards path; `patientEducation.taglish[]` in `backend/src/lib/cds/solver-content.ts`, 25 Taglish strings across 5 solver entries keyed by solver id (not ICD-10), carrying no reviewer, no review date, and no citation, reachable only from the retired `handlePatientCards`; and `backend/src/lib/cds/patient-card.ts`'s `preferredLanguage: 'english' | 'taglish'` on that same retired path. None of the three is corpus-backed and none is reachable from `findPatientEducationForDiagnosis`.
- Decision:
  - Exactly two registers exist: English and Taglish. There is no separate Tagalog register and no third register.
  - No third register is added anywhere — not to the CSV schema, not to `contracts/openapi.yaml`, not to any TypeScript type.
  - No stored identifier is renamed. `filipino` stays the value of `CdsPatientEducationSection.language`, the six `*_fil` CSV column names, `BilingualText.filipino`, and the ingest script's required-column list — a rename would be a contract change with no patient-visible benefit.
  - The patient-facing display label is corrected from "Filipino" to "Taglish" in exactly two places in `PatientEducationCard.tsx` (the toggle button label and the unavailable-register hint), per task 19.
  - The label/identifier divergence is documented at four points so a future reader finds the explanation instead of "fixing" the inconsistency: the doc comment above `type Language` in `PatientEducationCard.tsx`, this ADR, the Schema_Document's register section (task 20.3), and Property 29 in the test suite (task 19.3).
  - `CdsPatientEducationPayload.language`'s `bilingual` member is unchanged and still accurate: it denotes exactly the English register and this same Taglish register, two registers whatever the second is called.
  - The three unrelated `taglish` sightings in `cds.ts`, `solver-content.ts`, and `patient-card.ts` (all on the retired patient-cards path) are not promoted into this corpus and are not renamed by this decision.
- Alternatives considered:
  - Rename the stored identifier from `filipino` to `taglish` everywhere: rejected because it requires a `contracts/openapi.yaml` enum change (forbidden — Requirement 7.15), regenerated types, six CSV column renames, a `BilingualText` field rename, an update to the ingest script's required-column list, and every `=== "filipino"` comparison in the Splice — all for a value a patient never sees, since a patient only sees the rendered label.
  - Add a third register, a true Tagalog register distinct from the code-switched Taglish already stored: rejected because no such content exists, no clinician has reviewed or signed off on any such content, and adding one would require 120 new clinical strings, a fresh physician sign-off, an ADR-20260804-01 amendment to its bilingual approval condition, `BilingualText` becoming a triple, six new CSV columns, and a three-way frontend toggle — solving a problem that does not exist.
  - Leave the display label as "Filipino" and treat this as a documentation-only fix: rejected because the label is what a patient reads and acts on, and leaving it inaccurate costs a patient an incorrect expectation about what they are about to read, which outweighs the near-zero cost of changing two rendered strings.
  - Promote the retired patient-cards path's `taglish[]` solver strings (`solver-content.ts`) into the corpus as a shortcut to a "real" third register: rejected because that content has no reviewer, no review date, and no citation, and ADR-20260804-01 makes citation mandatory and review status a visibility gate for this corpus — promoting unreviewed content here is the exact substitution the assessment-first design exists to prevent.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: the patient-facing label is now accurate; the identifier stays stable so no contract, CSV, or Splice code changes; the divergence is documented at four points rather than left as a silent trap.
  - Trade-offs: a reader unfamiliar with this ADR who greps for "Taglish" in the backend will find only prose and comments, not the stored identifier, which is `filipino` — this is why the divergence is documented at four points instead of one.
  - Follow-up actions: none required; this is a documentation and one small frontend label change, fully closed by task 21.1's final register-scope assertion.
- Related files: `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/components/patientEducation/PatientEducationCard.tsx`, `frontend/bayan-health-mvp/src/features/consultation/components/patientEducation/PatientEducationCard.test.tsx`, `architecture/PATIENT_EDUCATION_CORPUS_SCHEMA.md`, `architecture/patient_education_corpus_v1.csv`, `architecture/patient_education_corpus_v2.csv`, `contracts/openapi.yaml`, `backend/src/handlers/cds.ts`, `backend/src/lib/cds/solver-content.ts`, `backend/src/lib/cds/patient-card.ts`

### ADR-20260828-01: Scheduled-request accept/decline, and a doctor dashboard rebuild that surfaced it needed one

- Date: 2026-08-28
- Status: Accepted — implemented locally behind a disabled-by-default flag; not applied to any environment; not qualified
- Owner: Lead Engineer (Dev A)
- Context: The doctor dashboard's old three-column board was being replaced by purpose-built cards (today strip, ready-to-start, upcoming-today, a trimmed incoming-requests card, a date-windowed completed-consultations card). That rebuild surfaced a gap in what a doctor could actually do about a `scheduled` booking: `doctorHoldingReservedSlot` (`matcher.ts`) auto-assigns the doctor the moment a patient pays for one of that doctor's published slots, and the doctor had never affirmatively agreed to *that patient* — only to publishing the time as bookable. There was no accept/decline step, and no cancel+refund path a doctor could use to say no to a specific scheduled booking without also eating a payment dispute later. On-demand bookings already had an analogous accept step via the request pool (ADR-20260805-04); scheduled bookings had nothing equivalent because auto-assignment was designed as a terminal step, not a proposal.
- Decision:
  - **New module `lib/scheduled-requests.ts`** exposes `listScheduledRequests`, `acceptScheduledRequest`, and `declineScheduledRequest`, gated end-to-end by `SCHEDULED_REQUEST_RESPONSE_ENABLED` (unset/non-`"true"` = disabled, routes 404, matching every other feature flag's exact-string convention in this codebase).
  - **A scheduled request is not a new entity.** It is a `confirmed`, `bookingMode = scheduled` booking with `intakeQueueStatus = 'pending'` (the value `assignDoctorToBooking` already sets on assignment) and no `acceptedAt` yet — addressable through the same GSI2 `DOCTOR#<id>` partition `listDoctorIntakeQueue` already queries. No new keys, no new GSIs, no `DATA_MODEL.md` change.
  - **Accept** sets `acceptedAt` and lands `intakeQueueStatus` on `ready`/`pending` by the same submitted-intake rule `claimOnDemandRequest` already applies, conditional on the caller being the assigned doctor, the booking still `confirmed`/`scheduled`/unaccepted, and pre-consultation (`attribute_not_exists(consultationId)`).
  - **Decline is fail-closed and strictly ordered, not a single transaction**, because a payment-gateway call cannot join a `TransactWriteCommand`: (1) a consistent-read preflight re-validates the same condition accept guards; (2) `refundPaymentForBooking` is attempted first, and its own `#st = :held` condition is the idempotency gate for a repeated decline; (3) only once the refund commits does the booking get cancelled and stamped with `declinedBy`/`declinedAt`/`declineReason` (note truncated to 500 chars), conditional on still being exactly the state preflight observed; (4) the slot is released back to `available` last, best-effort, conditional on it still naming this booking. A refund failure throws `ScheduledRequestRefundFailedError`, surfaced as `409 PAYMENT_NOT_REFUNDABLE`, and leaves the booking completely untouched — still `confirmed`, still assigned — rather than cancelling without a refund.
  - **The doctor's decline note is in-app-only by construction, not by convention.** `buildMessage`'s `booking_declined_by_doctor` case takes only `bookingId`, the same signature every other notification template uses — there is no parameter through which the note could reach an email/SMS body or a log line even by a future careless edit.
  - **One notification per cancellation, not two.** `declineScheduledRequest` stamps `declinedBy` atomically with `status = cancelled` in the same `UpdateCommand`, and `handleDynamoStreamRecord`'s cancellation branch checks for that field to choose `booking_declined_by_doctor` over the generic `booking_cancelled` — never both for the same write.
  - **Defect found and fixed in the same change, not deferred**: auto-assignment stamps `intakeQueueStatus = 'pending'` unconditionally, and `listDoctorIntakeQueue`'s pre-existing filter never excluded a freshly-assigned, not-yet-accepted scheduled booking. With the flag on, that booking would have appeared on *both* the new scheduled-requests surface *and* the pre-existing "Incoming Requests" card at once, and a doctor could have used the old `confirm`/`reject` intake-queue actions as a side door — `reject` in particular does not refund, so it could cancel a scheduled booking and unassign the doctor while leaving the patient's payment held. Both `listDoctorIntakeQueue` (excludes) and `processIntakeQueueItem` (refuses with `NOT_IN_INTAKE_QUEUE`) now share one predicate, `isUnrespondedScheduledRequest`, gated on the same flag so a disabled feature changes nothing.
- Alternatives considered:
  - A DynamoDB transaction spanning the refund and the cancel: rejected. `refundPaymentForBooking` calls out to the gateway adapter (ledger or PayRex), which cannot participate in a `TransactWriteCommand`; ordering with an idempotent refund-first gate is the closest correct sequencing available.
  - Importing `isScheduledRequestResponseEnabled` from `scheduled-requests.ts` into `intake-queue.ts` directly: rejected. `scheduled-requests.ts` imports `refundPaymentForBooking` from `lib/payments.ts`, which imports `finalizeBookingAfterPaymentConfirm` from `lib/intake-queue.ts` — importing the flag back would close a three-file cycle. Duplicated the one-line flag read instead, the same trade already made for the slot-release helpers duplicated from `handlers/bookings.ts`.
  - Splitting list+accept and decline into genuinely separate modules to match the two-task plan structure: rejected. Both share the identical preflight-condition shape and the identical "awaiting first response" definition; one cohesive module with one shared predicate is less likely to drift than two.
- Consequences:
  - Positive: a doctor can now decline a scheduled booking with a guaranteed refund before cancellation, with an optional note the patient sees in-app only, and the two competing surfaces for "things needing my decision" cannot double-list or race each other.
  - Positive: the found double-listing/side-door defect is closed before the flag is ever turned on anywhere, rather than being discovered live.
  - Trade-offs: a residual reconciliation window exists between a committed refund and a cancel write that loses its precondition (e.g. a `start` racing in) — logged as `decline_cancel_after_refund_conflict` for reconciliation rather than silently retried, since the refund must not be attempted twice. Slot release is best-effort and can leave a slot marked `booked` with no bookingId reconciliation path beyond the `decline_slot_release_reconciliation_required` log line.
  - **Not qualified.** `SCHEDULED_REQUEST_RESPONSE_ENABLED` defaults to `"false"` in the shared Terraform module and is left unset/`"false"` in every environment's own configuration, including dev — deliberately, since the frontend accept/decline UI, patient-side decline visibility, and rebooking wizard had not landed yet when the routes were wired. Backend `typecheck`, `lint`, and `test` (93 files / 1199 tests, all passing, zero regressions) were run. No `terraform apply`, no staging or production exposure.
